Current price of oil as of September 18, 2026
2026-09-18

Current price of oil as of September 18, 2026

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
At 8 a.m. Eastern Time on September 18, 2026, oil was priced at $104.33 per barrel with Brent serving as the benchmark (we’ll explain different benchmarks later in this article). That’s a gain of 35 cents compared with yesterday morning and around $37 higher than the price one year ago. It’s impossible to forecast oil prices with detailed precision. Many different elements affect the market, but ultimately it boils down to supply and demand. When worries about economic recession, war, and other large-scale disruptions increase, oil’s path can shift fast. Gas prices at the pump don’t only track crude oil. They also include what it takes to refine and move that fuel, the taxes layered on top, and the extra markup your local station adds to stay in business. Since crude oil generally makes up a majority of the per-gallon cost, changes in its price have an outsized impact. When oil surges, gas prices typically rise in tandem. But when oil retreats, gas prices often lag on the way down, a trend sometimes described as “rockets and feathers.” In case of emergency, the U.S. has a store of crude oil known as the Strategic Petroleum Reserve. Its primary purpose is energy security in case of disaster (think sanctions, severe storm damage, even war). But it can also go a long way toward softening crippling price hikes during supply shocks. It’s not a long-term answer and is more meant to provide temporary relief, assisting consumers and keeping critical parts of the economy running, like key industries, emergency services, public transportation, etc. Both oil and natural gas are key sources of the energy we use every day. Because of this, a big change in oil prices can affect natural gas. For example, if oil prices increase, some industries may swap natural gas for some segments of their operations where possible, which increases demand for natural gas. To gauge oil’s performance, we often turn to two benchmarks: Between these two, Brent better represents global oil performance because it prices much of the world’s traded crude. And, it’s often the best way to track historical oil performance. In fact, even the U.S. Energy Information Administration now uses Brent as its primary reference in its Annual Energy Outlook. Looking at the Brent benchmark across several decades, oil has been anything but steady. It’s seen spikes due to factors such as wars and supply cuts, and it’s also seen crashes from global recessions and an oversupply (called a “glut”). For example: All to say, oil’s historical performance has been anything but smooth. Again, it’s hugely affected by wars, recessions, OPEC whims, evolving energy initiatives and policies, and much more. Looking to stay up-to-date regarding the latest energy developments? Check out our recent coverage: The current price of oil per barrel depends largely on supply and demand, including news about potential future supply and demand (geopolitics, decisions made by OPEC+, etc.). In the U.S., prices also move based on how friendly an administration is to drilling, as it can affect future supply. For example, 2025 saw the Trump administration move to reopen more than 1.5 million acres in the Coastal Plain of the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge for oil and gas leasing, reversing the Biden administration’s policy of limiting oil drilling in the Arctic. The price of oil updates constantly when the “futures” markets are open. A futures market is effectively an auction where people agree to buy or sell oil in the future. As long as people and companies are trading contracts, the oil price is changing. In short, shale is rock that contains oil and natural gas. Think of shale as energy yet to be tapped. The more shale the U.S. accesses, the more energy we’ll have—and the more easily oil prices can keep from spiking as much thanks to a greater supply. When oil is expensive, it tends to make everyday items cost more. This can be related to energy (your heating, gas utilities, etc.), but it’s also due to the logistics involved with making those items accessible to you. Shipping, for example, can affect the price of things at the grocery store, as it’s more expensive to get those products from warehouses and farms onto the shelf. Warren Buffett’s last lesson as chair: Culture and capital require different guardians
2026-09-21

Warren Buffett’s last lesson as chair: Culture and capital require different guardians

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Good morning. At age 96, Warren Buffett has officially closed the book on his more than six-decade tenure as chairman of Berkshire Hathaway. Buffett stepped down as chairman on Sept. 18, effective immediately, and was succeeded by his son, Howard G. Buffett, a Berkshire director for 33 years. The move caps a leadership transition that began when Greg Abel took over as CEO on Jan. 1. While the CEO succession unfolded over a longer timeline, Buffett’s decision to relinquish the chairmanship came as a surprise to some investors, even though Berkshire described it as part of a “long-standing succession plan.” Buffett will remain on Berkshire’s board as chairman emeritus. In his shareholder letter, Buffett wrote that the timing was right to complete the transition, in part because Abel had exceeded his expectations, which were “sky-high from the start.” Buffett drew a sharp distinction between operating authority and cultural stewardship: “Greg runs the company; Howard will guard its culture and values,” he wrote, adding that both are “worth more than anything on our balance sheet.” It’s a governance model that separates the CEO’s operating mandate from the chairman’s role as culture custodian. Abel called Buffett’s impact on Berkshire “without parallel in the history of American business,” adding that the culture Buffett built “will remain at the heart of Berkshire, and Howard will be their guardian.” Susan Decker will remain continue as lead independent director. Abel’s imprint is already visible in Berkshire’s capital allocation. The company spent $39.4 billion on equity purchases in the first half of 2026, more than five times the $7.1 billion deployed in the same period a year earlier. Alphabet is now Berkshire’s third-biggest holding, worth $37.8 billion at midyear, after Abel added 48.1 million shares, part of a $10 billion commitment to support Alphabet’s AI investments, a new area for the conglomerate. That sits alongside Abel’s $6.8 billion purchase of homebuilder Taylor Morrison, a typical value bet in the Buffett mold, and $4.5 billion in share buybacks that resumed after more than a year of inactivity. The real test of Berkshire’s succession will not be whether Abel can imitate Buffett. It will be whether he can preserve Berkshire’s discipline while making capital-allocation decisions that Buffett himself might not have made. Todd Garner was appointed EVP and CFO of Avantor (NYSE: AVTR), effective Sept. 21, succeeding Steve Eck, who had served as interim CFO since June 24 and will continue as SVP and chief accounting officer. Garner joins Avantor from CONMED Corporation, where he served as EVP and CFO. Earlier in his career, he held leadership roles at C. R. Bard, including VP of investor relations and division CFO. James Sullivan was appointed SVP of finance and CFO of QuickLogic Corporation (Nasdaq: QUIK), semiconductor company, effective Oct. 5, succeeding Elias Nader, who resigned effective Sept. 18 to pursue other opportunities. Sullivan most recently served as CFO of Peraso Inc. He previously spent 14 years as CFO of MoSys, Inc., and held earlier CFO roles at Apptera, Inc. and 8x8, Inc. Sullivan began his career at PricewaterhouseCoopers. Capital One’s 2026 U.S. Middle Market Mid-Year Snapshot finds that many mid-sized U.S. companies are shifting from years of cost-cutting toward a greater focus on revenue growth, despite continued challenges from inflation, economic uncertainty, and cybersecurity risks. Based on a survey of 1,001 financial decision-makers at companies with $20 million to $2 billion in annual revenue, the report says growth-oriented businesses tend to invest more in talent, innovation, customer relationships, technology, and strategic planning. Meanwhile companies that remain focused on cost discipline are pulling back from these areas. The report also highlights the growing role of AI and automation, emphasizing that technology investments need to be paired with employee training and skills development. In addition, businesses increasingly view banks as strategic partners for areas such as financing, working capital, growth planning, and risk management. Overall, the report finds that middle-market companies as cautiously optimistic, with many responding to uncertainty not by slowing down but by adapting their strategies and investing in future growth. Wharton's Ripple Effect podcast digs into a structural risk lurking beneath private credit's rapid growth: the mismatch between what investors are promised and what funds can actually deliver on demand. Finance professor Itay Goldstein explains why funds investing in illiquid assets can face a dangerous gap when redemption requests spike, using the recent pressure on funds with software-company exposure and shifting AI-related investor sentiment as live examples of how declining inflows can magnify a liquidity crunch. "These jobs are super competitive. I mean, we have 20 to 30 spots, and we’ll get 5,000 applicants." —Laura Fuentes, Hilton’s chief human resources officer, told Fortune in an interview. The company's program is designed to give young workers a broad view of the business while setting them up for long-term careers. After their successful two-year immersion into the business, Launch participants are offered a full-time job. Silicon Republic
When should a STEM undergrad start to consider a post-grad?
2026-09-21

When should a STEM undergrad start to consider a post-grad?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
A master’s or post-grad isn’t always necessary for a long career, but how do you know when it is the logical next step? Master’s and post-grad programmes are designed to give students an advanced and often specialised education, so when the time comes to begin a career, they have the necessary skillset and qualifications. But not every STEM career requires an additional degree. More and more organisations are hiring based on talent and suitability, not just on where you went to college or the courses you undertook. Some people may want to jump straight into their professional life after college and others may feel that they need a little more support, guidance and knowledge before taking that step. So, with that in mind, when might another educational programme be the right move for an individual looking to pursue a career in a STEM field? In almost every industry, once an undergraduate degree is completed, most people have the skills and qualifications they need to start applying for jobs in their chosen field and often do so. Many large STEM companies specifically have internship programmes designed to support the career ambitions of recent graduates, so they can learn the ins and outs of the business. But sometimes, people may finish an undergraduate degree in the STEM space and not have the specifics required to move into the role they actually want. They might find that their previous education, while informative, was perhaps too broad for them to appeal to recruiters or employers. Post-grad programmes are an ideal way to significantly deepen your knowledge of often overlooked or niche fields, helping you stand out from the crowd when it comes to job-hunting – or even applying for a PhD. A more specialised education in AI and how it impacts other STEM fields, for example, can often be a game-changer for professionals, as evidenced by a recent report that indicated more than half of AI-related role postings are looking for skills beyond what the job title suggests, resulting in new hybrid roles. Life-changing career opportunities for you Build a future you believe in. Join our team A master’s or post-grad programme can give you access to a wider range of skills and future career routes, but it can also set you up for a role that offers greater remuneration in terms of titles and financial compensation. While an organisation might accept both undergraduate students and those who have graduated from a master’s programme around the same time for similar roles, jobseekers with additional qualifications can make a stronger argument for why their benefits package and pay should be greater than that of someone with less experience or education. Whether it is warranted or not, an employer may also consider candidates with a master’s for promotions and added responsibilities ahead of someone without one, simply as a policy and not necessarily in relation to the work already being carried out. It may also be a financially sound opportunity in the moment if you currently don’t have the means to continue your education, as many post-graduate programmes come with funding, particularly those in the STEM space. Funded post-graduate education or research positions are not always easy to secure and they often require a significant commitment. Therefore, having earned and completed one can also show an employer that you are competitive, hard-working and focused. It may well be that after you have completed your undergraduate education, you feel as though you have far more to contribute. A post-graduate degree gives you the chance to carry out your own research and test your own theories, with the hope of having a paper or results to publish in the end. It is an ideal opportunity to make your mark in the STEM space and can open up many doors post-college; for example, it can lead to a career in academia, or can be a great stepping stone towards a STEM-focused company that depends on forward-thinking and innovative people. At the end of the day, choosing to undertake an additional degree after you have completed your undergraduate course is all a matter of circumstance. It is highly dependent on your current skillset, the career and title you are aiming for, the lifestyle you would prefer – but also what you can feasibly afford and commit to now. While it might sound like a lot to consider, once you have, your decision should be relatively easy. And remember, you can always go back to college, regardless of where you are at in your professional life – the door is always open. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Scientists bet on 3D-printed coral reefs to revitalise marine life
2026-09-21

Scientists bet on 3D-printed coral reefs to revitalise marine life

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
University of Galway researchers attaching coral to the artificial reef. Image: Redecor ‘We cannot simply wait for them to recover on their own,’ says Ifremer expert Marie-Claire Fabri. Scientists are hoping 3D-printed coral reefs can help revive deep sea habitats destroyed by bottom trawling. Researchers from the University of Galway, Sorbonne University and the French oceanographic institution Ifremer, are testing 23 artificial reefs placed on the seabed off the south-west Porcupine Bank – 200km off the west coast of Ireland – and in the Bay of Biscay, France. The tests are a part of a three-year joint collaboration between the three universities called Restauration Des Coraux d’eau froide (Redecor). As part of the mission, scientists were deployed on an expedition aboard a vessel from the French Oceanographic Fleet this August, where they placed 3D-printed reefs at depths of between 900m and 1100m off the Porcupine Bank and in the Guilvinec Canyon, Bay of Biscay. Despite being lesser known than their tropical counterparts, cold-water coral reefs provide important habitats for marine life and support biodiversity in the deep sea. Deep sea trawling, however, has damaged coral life. And despite a ban on trawling in many coral-rich areas, the fragile habitat requires additional efforts to revive to full health. “These deep-sea coral reefs provide vital habitat for a diversity of marine life, but many have been damaged over many years,” said Dr Anthony Grehan, a deep-sea ecologist at University of Galway and the co-leader of the mission to Porcupine Bank. “Because these corals grow by only a few millimetres each year, natural recovery of lost reefs can take decades to centuries. “With this project, we are testing whether we can actively accelerate that recovery by creating new starter structures for corals to grow on which also mimic reef structure to help re-establish functioning reef habitats.” Testing artificial reefs as a viable method to revitalise sea life is one of the ways scientists in the bloc are attempting to tackle marine degradation as part of a four-year Horizon Europe project called ‘Restoration of deep-sea habitats to rebuild European Seas’, or Redress. The artificial reefs are eco-designed cylinders made from low-carbon concrete incorporating volcanic material, University of Galway explained. They are placed individually on the seabed to form a single connected structure covering an area of about half a hectare. Each 3D-printed structure is around 80cm high and 100cm in diameter and weighs around 600kg. It provides physical microhabitats such as tunnels, ledges and overhangs for corals and other marine life to colonise. “The early results are very encouraging, and if we can show that these approaches work, it could open up new possibilities to scale up the restoration of damaged coral reefs elsewhere in the Atlantic and beyond,” said Grehan. Scientists are using different approaches to bring the seabed back to life. Some of the seabeds being tested are equipped with oyster shells, ceramic plates and tiles to encourage coral larvae to settle naturally, while others were fitted with fragments of living coral. These live coral fragments were collected from the ocean at a similar depth to the artificial ones and kept in seawater tanks under conditions designed to replicate their natural environment. Small branches were then taken from the colonies and attached to oyster shells before being anchored on the artificial reefs. The researchers found that the fragments placed on artificial reefs last year are alive and have been colonised by a range of marine species, including sea urchins, fish and crabs. Both restoration sites are protected from any further damage by bottom trawling, but that is not enough, said Marie-Claire Fabri, a research engineer in deep-sea benthic ecology at Ifremer and the co-leader of the Bay of Biscay region expedition, who called for active restoration efforts. “The ban on bottom trawling has helped stop the direct pressure on these habitats, but to go further – given the slow growth of these corals in the Atlantic – just a few millimetres per year – we cannot simply wait for them to recover on their own.” Scientists will continue to monitor these sites over the coming years, and work on better understanding their exact marine conditions to help better replicate the lost habitats. Redress brings 27 partner organisations from 15 countries to investigate the restoration of damaged deep-sea habitats across Europe. The EU-funded project also has similar artificial reef modules in restoration sites across the Atlantic and the Mediterranean. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Related: Horizon Europe, EU, Galway, Europe, University of Galway, Ireland, marine life Suhasini Srinivasaragavan is a sci-tech reporter for Silicon Republic
Silicon Republic
Are internships a stepping stone to future careers the way degrees are?
2026-09-21

Are internships a stepping stone to future careers the way degrees are?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Matthew J Mayhew, Courtney DeRoche and Yun-Han Weng of Ohio State University discuss whether an internship or a degree is the correct choice for career growth. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) College students are facing one of the most precarious labour markets in years. A recent New York Federal Reserve study pointed to a sharp increase in the post-college unemployed compared with other workers, with explanations ranging from artificial intelligence job displacement to a broader slowdown in hiring. One response has been a boom in trade-school and apprenticeship programmes that launch young people into ‘AI-proof’ careers. But students at community and four-year colleges can also beat these headwinds and launch a career through on-the-job internships. We’re a group of multidisciplinary scholars at The Ohio State University who study two paid internship programmes in science, technology, engineering and math, or STEM, with a proven record of landing jobs for students. Although the two initiatives use different models, they both illustrate four lessons that colleges and employers can use to turn internships into genuine career pathways. Just as notable is that a large share of these students come from diverse and underrepresented backgrounds, with many being the first in their families to go to college. These programmes can serve as a powerful equaliser for young people who may not otherwise have such career opportunities. Internships remain one of higher education’s most powerful tools for preparing students for work. Decades of research show that students who complete high-quality internships are more likely to secure job interviews and high-earning positions post-graduation. At the same time, the number of internship opportunities is diminishing, even though that preparation matters more than ever. Artificial intelligence is transforming entry-level work, leading employers to increasingly value the human skills that machines cannot easily replicate: judgement, adaptability, collaboration and the ability to solve unfamiliar problems. Academic leaders are recognising these pressures. More than one-third of university presidents and two-thirds of community college presidents told an Inside Higher Ed survey that they plan to add or expand apprenticeship pathways over the next three years. But it’s not just the number of internships that matters – internship quality counts just as much. Our research at the College Impact Laboratory examined learning through experience with two multi-institutional studies. One is Bridging Academic Training Through Experiential Research and Innovation, or BATTERI, led by principal investigator Jay Sayre and project manager Joanna Gardner. The other is Drive to Thrive. Across both initiatives, students benefited when their experiences combined substantive work with mentoring and professional development, rather than being treated as inexpensive labour. This distinction suggests that merely placing students in workplaces isn’t enough. The structure and quality of those experiences help determine whether internships lead to meaningful, high-earning careers. Drive to Thrive deploys an approach called ‘earn-as-you-learn’. Through partnerships with Ford dealerships, students alternate between classroom instruction and paid workplace experience. They work alongside experienced technicians, diagnose and repair customers’ vehicles and receive regular coaching. In this sense, it’s similar to the European model of traineeships for young people. This initiative reached 635 students at 18 community colleges, including 345 first-generation students and 300 students of colour. Through interviews and focus groups, a majority told us the internship helped them see themselves as true professionals. And several said the internship confirmed they had chosen the right career. The numbers also backed up our approach. Students in the earn-as-you-learn programme were almost 50pc more likely to graduate compared with students in a matched comparison group. Our other programme brings together clean energy manufacturing students from three different types of institutions – a major research university, a historically Black university and a community college – for a 10-week internship. The programme combines workplace learning with professional development workshops, peer networking, community engagement and projects with industry partners such as Honda, Stellantis and Schaeffler. This initiative took a distinctive approach by assigning multiple mentors to each student. The idea is to help students develop relationships with faculty members, technical experts, employers and classmates. This network, in turn, strengthened students’ confidence, professional identity and sense of belonging while complementing the technical skills they develop. To date, the formula has worked. More than half the participants in 2025 have already lined up either a new internship or job interviews. Industry partners also plan to rehire several members of the 2026 cohort as interns, extending their training beyond our programme and opening pathways into clean-tech careers. Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Unlocking the potential of biology for patients Based on this record, we believe that colleges should build broader support networks around internships rather than expect a single supervisor to shoulder the entire responsibility for a student’s professional development. In both programmes, four valuable principles emerged. First is the importance of meaningful work. We believe interns should tackle real organisational challenges with deliverables that matter beyond the classroom. For example, two interns spent the summer addressing a safety challenge for Honda – how to prevent battery cells from catching fire and cooling them down when they do. After developing potential solutions, they presented their work to industry representatives. A senior engineer from the Honda Research Institute was so impressed that he contacted them about a post-internship opportunity. That sense of consequence changed how the students approached the work. Rather than viewing it as an assignment to complete, they were making a contribution that others depended on. Second is the constellation of support for students. The liaison between the institution and industry partners is essential, serving as the connective tissue that holds the internship together and ensuring that students don’t fall through the cracks. But that liaison alone isn’t enough. Students also need access to faculty, industry supervisors, technical experts and peers, since each brings a different kind of guidance. One strength of the clean energy programme, for example, is its inclusion of industry experts across institutions and organisations. Students benefit most when they can draw on multiple mentors and peer support to develop key collaborative skills. Third, students should learn how to normalise struggle. Internships aren’t a test of whether students can already function as professionals. They’re environments for students to try, fail and retry. When educators emphasise struggle as part of the learning process, students are encouraged to avoid perfectionism and instead ask for help when difficulties arise. For example, students submit weekly reports during the clean energy programme that give them the chance to discuss challenges, troubleshoot problems together and recognise that uncertainty is part of becoming a professional. Finally, it’s vital to make interns’ contributions visible. Presentations, project showcases and recognition from industry professionals reinforce that students’ work matters while strengthening their professional identities. We believe universities and industries alike should heed these lessons, which extend well beyond clean energy manufacturing and automotive technology. Successful programs build ecosystems that often determine whether students simply complete an internship or launch a career. As colleges respond to changing labour markets, they should resist measuring success just by how many internships they create. Instead, they should ask whether those opportunities help students become confident, capable professionals. Internship quantity opens the door to better outcomes after college. Internship quality changes lives. By Matthew J Mayhew, Courtney DeRoche and Yun-Han Weng Prof Matthew J Mayhew is a professor of higher education at The Ohio State University. He is interested in how collegiate conditions, educational practices and student experiences influence learning and democratic outcomes, including: moral reasoning; pluralism; productive exchange across worldview differences; and innovation capacity. To support the study of college and its impact on student development and learning, Mayhew has been awarded more than $16m in funding from sources including but not limited to the US Department of Education, the Ewing Marion Kauffman Foundation, the Andrew C Mellon Foundation, the Fetzer Institute and the Merrifield Family Foundation. Dr Courtney DeRoche is a postdoctoral researcher at The Ohio State University’s College Impact Laboratory, where she leads the ‘Drive to Thrive: Student Success in Career and Technical STEM’ project. Yun-Han Weng is a postdoctoral researcher at The Ohio State University. Her research focuses on collegiate environments, STEM education and internationalisation. She has been in the field for 10 years and has studied how different interventions and different collegiate climates impact students’ sense of belonging and learning outcomes. Google fined €403m by DPC over GDPR violations
2026-09-21

Google fined €403m by DPC over GDPR violations

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Users could have been unaware that their data was being used for ads, said DPC deputy commissioner Graham Doyle. Ireland’s data protection watchdog has fined Google €403m and ordered the tech giant to become compliant with the region’s privacy regulations within six months. The Data Protection Commission (DPC), concluding a six-year-long probe, found that Google did not comply with the EU’s GDPR when processing data generated in the bloc. Its inquiry, triggered by complaints from European consumer rights organisations, including from BEUC, probed how the company processed location data across its ‘web & app activity’, ‘location history’ and ‘location accuracy’ features between May 2018 and February 2020. ‘Web & app activity’ is a Google account setting that processes information related to users’ activity on Google services, including sites and apps, meanwhile ‘location history’ tracks users’ locations and processes the data as visited places, activities and paths between locations. The complaints raised concerns relating to the “legality of Google’s processing of location data and the transparency surrounding that processing”, the DPC said in a statement announcing the probe in 2020. The probe found that Google infringed the GDPR by failing to lawfully process its location data, failing to demonstrate its compliance with the GDPR and failing to meet its transparency obligations across the features in question. “Location data can bring both benefits and harms to individuals,” said DPC deputy commissioner Graham Doyle. “It can greatly enhance the utility of online services, but it can also reveal a significant amount of information about an individual, including information that is inherently private. “The GDPR provides a high level of protection of personal data throughout the EEA, and requires that the processing of personal data must be carried out in a lawful, fair and transparent manner. “As a result of Google’s failures in this regard, individuals could have been unaware that their location was being used to, for example, influence them with ads or to infer their interests, and could lose control over their personal data. The retention of users’ location data for longer than necessary aggravated this loss of control.” In a statement to SiliconRepublic.com, a Google spokesperson said that the case centres around “historical policies” that have been updated since. “From 2019 onwards, we’ve significantly evolved our practices and launched robust tools that make managing location data simple,” the company said. In 2019, Google introduced auto-delete controls that allow users to automatically delete data on a rolling three-, 18- or 36-month basis. In 2023, it updated its location history feature to enable timelines to be stored directly on users’ devices. Google also said it does not store precise location data in ‘web & app activity’ and allows users to manage how their data is used for ads. This is the DPC’s fourth largest fine since the GDPR came into effect. In 2023, Meta received the largest fine issued by the watchdog to date, raking in €1.2bn in penalties after illegally transferring Facebook user data from the EU to the US. Last year, TikTok was fined €530m for transferring personal user data to China, while Meta was also subject to a separate €405m fine in 2022 for publicly disclosing contact details of children using Instagram’s business accounts. UK AI infrastructure start-up Nscale files for US IPO

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-21

Nscale’s data centre in Glomfjord, Norway. Image: Nscale The Financial Times reported that the company is seeking ‘a valuation of up to $35bn’. UK AI infrastructure developer Nscale has filed for a US initial public offering (IPO) through a statement of registration to the country’s Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC). The number of shares to be offered and their price range have not yet been determined, according to the company, which has applied to list its ordinary shares on the New York Stock Exchange under the ticker symbol ‘NSCL’. The Financial Times, however, reported on Friday (18 September) that Nscale is seeking “a valuation of up to $35bn” in the IPO, citing people familiar with the matter. Goldman Sachs, JP Morgan and Morgan Stanley will act as lead bookrunners for the proposed offering. RBC Capital Markets, Deutsche Bank Securities and TD Securities, among others, will act as other bookrunners, while Citizens Capital Markets, Loop Capital Markets and Roth Capital Partners, among others, will act as the offering’s co-managers, Nscale said. According to its SEC filing, the London-based AI start-up had a net loss of $1.02bn on revenue of $140.6m for the six months ending on 30 June; for the same period in 2025, it had a net loss of $368.9m on revenue of $10.4m. “As of 31 August 2026, our infrastructure portfolio included approximately 25,000 active GPUs and 461,000 active and contracted GPUs, five active and 12 contracted data centre sites (including seven wholly-owned sites, nine co-location sites and one leased site) and approximately 1.37GW of active and contracted capacity (representing 1GW at owned sites, 200MW at leased sites and 165MW at co-location sites),” Nscale’s filing read. It added that the company’s footprint “is concentrated in renewable-rich, low-cost power regions such as Norway, Portugal, Iceland and select locations in the United States and APAC”. The company raised $2bn in Series C funding at a valuation of $14.6bn in March of this year. At the time, it said the money would be used to accelerate its global development of vertically integrated AI infrastructure – including GPU compute, networking, data services and orchestration software – across Europe, North America and Asia. Nscale has AI infrastructure deployment deals with Microsoft and Nvidia; its founder, CEO and chair – Josh Payne – received total compensation in 2025 of $23.2m, according to last week’s SEC filing notice. Pensions Awareness Ireland launches AI retirement coach

The platform aims to give employees greater clarity and insight when it comes to planning for retirement. As part of its plans for Pensions Awareness Week 2026, Pensions Awareness Ireland has launched what it claims to be Ireland’s first AI retirement coach. The aim of the model, named Ali, is to empower employees to gain greater control and clarity over their financial future. Employees can then explore their options and start to implement any necessary changes they need to make in order to improve their long-term financial outlook. From there, dashboard users will receive an invitation to join a conversation with Ali, which has been trained on the Irish pensions system and is programmed to provide tailored responses based on the user’s dashboard. The new rules mean that eligible employees, who wish to do so, can now choose to remain employed at their place of work for a period of time beyond their contractual retirement age, where that age is below the State Pension age of 66. Nvidia CEO Promises Not to Kill Everybody Before 2030

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Earlier this month, disillusioned Anthropic employee Jacob Coxon publicly announced he was quitting his job, accusing both his employer and OpenAI of acting irresponsibly. His departure and harrowing warning that there’s a more than ten percent chance of AI “killing all humans” was met with breathless media coverage. While he’s far from the first AI insider to warn that humanity is in grave danger, the timing was particularly noteworthy. The public was already primed to expect the worst after a summer chock full of rogue AI models going on hacking sprees and infiltrating third party companies without their human creators’ knowledge. But not everybody’s convinced that our days as a species on Earth are counted. For one: chipmaker Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang — the man who’s effectively selling shovels during the ongoing AI gold rush — is pleading for a more rational and grounded discussion. “2030 is not going to be the end of the world,” he told CBS News, responding to Coxon’s claims. “There is zero chance chance that’s going to be the end of the world.” “Scaring people is unnecessary,” Huang added. “It is irresponsible.” While we should certainly take his personal point of view with an enormous grain of salt — after all, Huang is astronomically invested in the success of AI infrastructure sales — it’s nonetheless a moderately soothing take after weeks of widespread AI doomerism. But while a superintelligent swarm of AI models may not necessarily wipe out humanity any time soon, there are credible reasons to be concerned. World powers are exploiting the tech to develop autonomous machines of war, its unbridled use can lead to widespread “AI psychosis,” and data centers are actively harming an environment that’s already on the precipice of disaster. Then there’s an almost complete regulatory vacuum, with the Trump administration cringing at the mere thought of setting rules for an AI slowdown, something almost every major tech leader has been calling for lately. Huang, however, said he was aligned with US president Donald Trump’s incoherent argument against government guardrails, telling CBS that existing laws setting rules for liability and unauthorized entry are enough. “You have all kinds of liabilities associated with cybersecurity,” he said. “Apply that first — don’t let this doomsday narrative allow someone to relieve them of the laws that currently exist.” Huang also agreed with Trump that there’s a worldwide AI race we can’t ignore, particularly between the US and China. “Don’t you wish every country would have aspirations for a better tomorrow?” he told CBS. A Highly Trafficked Space News Site Invented a Fake NASA Engineer and Used Her Name to Publish to AI-Generated Slop

A Highly Trafficked Space News Site Invented a Fake NASA Engineer and Used Her Name to Publish to AI-Generated Slop

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
The space publication Space Daily, which has been operating for over three decades, has become an unrecognizable shell of its former self after a company called Brown Brothers Media bought it and turned it into a content mill that pumps out misleading and badly sourced AI slop. As a Futurism investigation found, Space Daily is averaging an estimated ten million page views per month. That would be an incredible success story — if it weren’t for the fact that it’s been platforming non-existent experts as puppets to churn out AI garbage. Case in point, one of the website’s authors, “Dr. Katherine Chen,” was identified as a former “systems engineer” at NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory who “spent fifteen years designing autonomous systems for deep space missions,” according to her since-deleted profile on the site. A different writer, listed as “Dr. James Whitfield” by Space Daily, was allegedly an “aerospace medicine researcher at the European Space Agency.” But Futurism confirmed that neither Chen nor Whitfield actually exist. Instead, Brown Brothers Media invented their personas to publish slop on a massive scale under the bylines of what looked like trustworthy experts. “Mr. James Whitfield is not currently employed by the European Space Agency, nor do our records indicate that he has ever worked here in the past,” the ESA told us in a statement. While NASA declined to comment, its website doesn’t make a single mention of a “Katherine Chen.” After we started investigating Brown Brothers Media, the company began mass-deleting vast numbers of articles. It also deleted many of the fake writers, including Chen and Whitfield. “Before May 2026, our publications used contributor bylines that did not identify real people, and some of those biographies included credentials that were not real,” the company conceded in a statement. “That was wrong. In May, we stopped publishing under those bylines, began removing or correcting the older articles involved, and published network-wide and per-site editorial standards. That review is ongoing.” After the mass culling, Space Daily is still publishing plenty of articles, albeit under shadowy bylines like “Space Daily Editorial Team,” or “Mission Notes.” Many of the articles claim to have been edited by either one of the Brown Brothers Media founders, a member of its editorial team, or a constellation of contributors. Its subsequent output hasn’t been inspiring. Its homepage prominently featured an awkwardly-written piece about an encounter between the ESA’s Rosetta spacecraft and Comet 67P — which happened over a decade ago. The only reason Space Daily is talking about it now, obviously, is to get clicks by flooding the zone in an attempt to trick Google’s algorithms. Even a brief perusal of the content being published raises red flags. A piece with the brain-meltingly long headline “A Trillion-Mile Stream of Gas Is Falling Onto a Triple Star, and It Lines Up With the Outermost of Its Three Tilted Rings to Within Three Degrees” is virtually incomprehensible, taking the form of a jumble of words about “angles,” “fitted numbers,” and “misalignments.” (Pangram found it to be entirely AI-generated, with no sign of human editing.) The article is bylined by Chris Jeremia, who appears to be a “meat proxy,” or an individual Brown Brothers Media uses by affixing their name to a fully AI-generated piece. Besides publishing articles about astrophysics and cuneiform clay tablets, Jeremia also is listed as the magazine editor of VegOut, and also finds time to cover beluga whales in Canada that face euthanasia for The Vessel — both other AI content mills run by Brown Brothers Media. She may exist in the real world, but Jeremia appears to have just as much editorial authority as Dr. Chen and Dr. Whitfield. Solar Power Is Getting So Cheap That It’s Almost Unbelievable
2026-09-20

Solar Power Is Getting So Cheap That It’s Almost Unbelievable

Futurism | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Though the price to install new solar panels has been trending downward for a while, it’s officially reached a new milestone: “offensively cheap.” Provocative new reporting by the Financial Times highlighted the new paradigm around photovoltaics, which are disrupting traditional energy networks with a near-infinite supply, resulting in shockingly low prices. As Dave Jones, co-founder of the energy research firm Ember, told the FT, solar panels used to run between $5 and $6 per watt of generation capacity. That was 26 years ago. Today, Jones says solar panels sell for just 12 cents per watt — a price he says is “offensively cheap” considering the alternatives. The FT characterizes that jaw-dropping price decrease as “entirely the result” of a massive rise in Chinese solar panel production, which is making renewable energy available to rich and developing countries alike for bottom-barrel prices. In rural parts of the world, this means that a “home goes from burning two or three kerosene lanterns to a light that is 20 to 40 times brighter, [turned] on every day,” Anish Thakkar, co-founder of Kenyan solar financing company Sun King told the paper. And as the barriers to enter a solar-powered world come down, the amount of energy generated by photovoltaics is reaching unprecedented heights. In China, the undisputed world-leader in renewable energy, new clean energy installations have flooded the grid with electricity faster than it can be used. As executive vice chairman of battery giant BTR New Material Group Youyuan Huang said in a forum covered by Fortune, “China’s grid is a very strong and stable one, but we’ve installed too much green energy.” In the US, the green energy build-out is likewise making headlines. Despite president Donald Trump’s efforts to revive the declining US coal industry, photovoltaics produced more electricity than coal-fired generators for the first time in June. And across the pond in Spain, the simply incredible amount of solar panel installations has flipped the domestic energy economy upside-down, resulting in a mass exodus of venture capital as customers have to be paid to use more power. All in all, there’s never been a better time to buy solar panels — which is especially good news as the crude oil market turns belly up. Some schoolboys are building online following by making this kind of Meta glasses content, too. Meta has responded to the unfolding PR crisis by saying it would ban the Instagram accounts of these content creators and brick the cameras of customers who’re caught using them inappropriately. Futurism
Scientists Claim That Three Minutes With a “Donkey or Small Horse” Can Save Teenagers From Depression
2026-09-20

Scientists Claim That Three Minutes With a “Donkey or Small Horse” Can Save Teenagers From Depression

Futurism | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Life got you feeling down? A donkey could be the solution to lifting your spirits. This is the gist of a new study published in the journal Science Reports, which found that teenagers who spent as little as three minutes petting and caressing rescued donkeys and miniature horses immediately felt less anxious, sad, and lonely, while also feeling calmer. It may not literally cure your depression, but the work shows that interacting with these animals can significantly enhance your mood. “It was lovely to see that interacting with nature could make such a positive difference so quickly,” study co-lead author Rebecca Calisi-Rodríguez, an associate professor of neurobiology, physiology and behavior at the University of California, Davis, told the Washington Post. The therapeutic potential of animals, of course, is nothing new. But the researchers wanted to focus on something that other studies on animal-assisted mental health interventions have overlooked: how the animals’ reacted to the different ways people treated them. “You can’t stand in a pasture watching a child and a donkey interact and pretend only one of them is having an experience,” Calisi-Rodríguez said in a statement about the work. “They’re watching each other, they’re responding to each other, they’re both making choices. That realization became the heart of my work and the heart of this paper.” To explore this, the researchers divided the 61 teenagers into two groups. One was told to gently stroke the donkeys’ and miniature horses’ backs, and the others were told to scratch their necks, which mimics how equines groom each other. All of them were taught to recognize two behavioral cues. If the donkey wriggles its lips, it’s feeling pleasure. If the donkey walks away, it wants to be left alone. Unsurprisingly, the donkeys wriggled their lips more during neck-scratching and usually walked away when they were petted on the back. The interesting bit is what this did for the teens. All of the teens reported feeling calmer regardless if they scratched or petted the animal. But the ones who saw the donkeys wriggle their lips experienced bigger decreases in loneliness, the study found. And even the ones whose donkeys walked away from them had bigger reductions in sadness. The important part, it seems, is being in tune with the animal. “Seeing an animal exercise choice — being free to approach you, stay with you, or walk away — may make the interaction feel more authentic,” Calisi Rodríguez said in the statement. “This really isn’t a story about the best way to pet a donkey, as cute as that would be. It’s a story about reciprocity,” she added. Remember: it’s therapy for the animals, too. Kenya Unleashing Solar-Powered Ambulance With Wicked Long Range for Service to Remote Villages
2026-09-20

Kenya Unleashing Solar-Powered Ambulance With Wicked Long Range for Service to Remote Villages

Futurism | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
A huge share of Kenya’s population lives in remote villages and townships, far away from the amenities offered by urban life. And though Kenya’s rural population may have shrunk in recent decades, it still encompasses the vast majority of its people, presenting a major challenge for the country’s struggling healthcare sector. One group of students from half a world away is hoping to help. As EuroNews reports, students at the Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands have designed a solar-powered electric ambulance, meant to improve emergency healthcare access to remote communities. Called the Stella Juva, the vehicle reportedly came about following discussions with non-government organizations working on improving healthcare access across Africa. The ambulance, which is decidedly Cybertruck-shaped, is designed to transport emergency medical supplies as well as equipment for basic medical-checkups, making it more like a health clinic on wheels. Per EuroNews, it comes equipped with an ultrasound scanner, X-ray equipment, a defibrillator, and a refrigerator for transporting vaccinations and other supplies. “It will enable us to go and screen mothers and babies, catch complications early and refer them in real time,” Geoffery Sadera, a project officer at the non-profit Amref Health Africa, told EuroNews. All together, the EV boasts a range of about just over 444 miles. It’s said to have a top speed of just shy of 75mph. The Juva is now being tested in select communities across Kenya to see how viable a fully solar-powered ambulance can be when deployed in real world conditions. As exciting a development as it is, Kenya still faces a long series of hurdles that no amount of buzzy EVs can solve. As a 2025 report by the Kenyan Office of the Auditor General observed, the country still lacks a centralized toll-free emergency number like 911, a symptom of the many systemic gaps plaguing Kenya’s public health system. As emergency dispatch is organized on a county-by-county basis, much of Kenya’s emergency services lack basic essentials like trained dispatchers, reliable internet service, and emergency backup power, let alone logistical systems like electronic call-logging and real-time response tracking. In its summary of the Auditor General’s report, the Emergency Medicine Kenya Foundation noted that only 26 percent of the public was aware of what number to dial in case of an emergency. Even when an ambulance is dispatched, the average national response time ranged from 20 minutes to an hour — and that’s for those counties which actually had dispatch data to share. “Emergency response in Kenya is largely uncoordinated, reactive, and dependent on geography rather than need,” the Foundation explained. And that’s all before unpacking the conditions at Kenya’s hospitals and clinics once a trauma patient arrives. Against this backdrop, a few solar-powered ambulances surely won’t hurt — but absent major reforms to Kenya’s healthcare system, they’re likely to amount to little more than a bandaid on a chronic wound. Jack Altman, Peter Fenton, Chetan Puttagunta, Everett Randle and Eric Vishria will come together for this main stage session, “What We Believe Now” – the first time the entire current Benchmark partnership has appeared together on the Disrupt Stage in San Francisco. Rather than another conversation about where venture capital has been, the focus is on where it goes next: where the next generation of startups will come from, which assumptions founders should reconsider, and which opportunities the partners believe are still hiding in plain sight. And 2026 is an especially interesting year to ask them. Benchmark has spent decades building a reputation around concentrated early-stage investing. This year, it made one of the biggest changes in its own playbook – raising approximately $2 billion across a $750 million flagship fund and its first $1.25 billion growth fund.  The market changed. Benchmark changed with it. Now we want to know what the firm thinks will happen next. Sit front and center to get tomorrow’s venture insights at Disrupt. AI has reshaped the venture market with extraordinary speed. According to the OECD, AI companies captured 61% of global venture capital investment in 2025 – $258.7 billion of $427.1 billion invested overall. Yet that capital was far from evenly distributed: deals worth more than $100 million represented roughly 73% of total AI investment value. That creates a strange environment for founders. There is huge appetite for technology businesses, alongside growing competition for a relatively small number of companies investors believe can become category leaders. Is the next billion-dollar company another AI application, or is the application layer already overcrowded? Does defensibility sit in models, infrastructure, proprietary data or distribution? Are some of the best businesses being overlooked because everyone is chasing the same themes? And when founders can build products faster than ever, what actually makes one company investable? There probably won’t be complete agreement onstage. That’s the point. Hear this complex discussion among five VC heavyweights on the Disrupt Stage. Benchmark’s partnership brings together experience from founding companies, backing enterprise software, investing in frontier technology, and helping businesses navigate IPOs and acquisitions. Jack Altman joined Benchmark this year after founding Lattice and subsequently building his own venture firm, Alt Capital. Before joining Benchmark, Alt Capital had raised $425 million across its early-stage investing activities. His path gives the discussion an unusually direct founder-to-investor perspective. Peter Fenton brings one of venture’s longest track records. His investments span consumer and enterprise companies, including current AI bets such as Sierra, Digits and Sema4.ai. He has served as a director through seven successful IPOs, including Twitter, Elastic, New Relic, Zendesk and Yelp. Chetan Puttagunta focuses on early-stage enterprise software and has backed companies including MongoDB, MuleSoft, Elastic, Modern Treasury, Legora and Stytch. Everett Randle brings experience investing across stages and categories, with investments that include Anthropic, SpaceX, Rippling, Flock Safety, Gumloop and Chainguard. And Eric Vishria focuses on early-stage infrastructure and enterprise software, with investments including Amplitude, Confluent, Fireworks.ai and Cerebras Systems. Before becoming an investor, he was himself a startup CEO, co-founding RockMelt before its acquisition by Yahoo. Put those perspectives together and this session, “What We Believe Now,” becomes less about a single Benchmark thesis and more about how experienced investors disagree, update their assumptions, and decide where conviction is warranted. Hear this session live in October and save up to $200 when you register before prices rise on September 25. Cerebras is a useful example. Vishria recently told TechCrunch that he almost didn’t take his first meeting with the AI chip startup in 2016. Hardware was outside Benchmark’s comfort zone, and what Cerebras wanted to build looked extraordinarily difficult. By the third slide, he had changed his mind. Benchmark went on to co-lead the company’s $25 million Series A. A decade later, Cerebras went public, with Benchmark holding a 9.5% stake at the IPO. That story encapsulates something important about venture investing. The best opportunity does not always arrive looking like the consensus winner. Sometimes it challenges the investor’s existing thesis. Sometimes the technology is too early. Sometimes the market does not obviously exist yet. And sometimes knowing when to change your mind is more valuable than being right from the beginning. Expect that kind of thinking to surface on the Disrupt Stage. Google’s $899 Googlebook is a bet that you’ll buy a new laptop for Gemini
2026-09-21

Google’s $899 Googlebook is a bet that you’ll buy a new laptop for Gemini

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt After first unveiling its new AI-powered laptop, dubbed Googlebook, in May, Google on Monday said the new device is now available for pre-order for $899. Meant to offer a step up from traditional Chromebooks, Googlebooks run on Android OS with a desktop version of the Chrome browser and other familiar ChromeOS elements. However, the real pull for the new devices is that they’ll prominently feature Gemini’s latest capabilities in a new format: the laptop. Google is trying to offer a laptop with unique features like an AI-powered cursor, vibe-coded widgets, and support for AI-enhanced dictation. The latter is a feature called Rambler, which cleans up messy, rambling brain dumps into readable text. The move is a bet that AI, specifically Google’s Gemini, could be enough of a draw to get people to buy a new laptop that bakes in AI instead of running them via desktop apps you install or access through the browser. In addition, the devices could eventually replace Chromebooks, bringing millions of former Chromebook customers to Gemini AI. On the Googlebook, Google is trying to reinvent the “point-and-click” experience of desktop computing to now include an AI element: the cursor serves as a conduit to Gemini. The company suggests various ways this could be used, like highlighting content on a webpage for Gemini to work with, asking Gemini to see if an email you’ve hovered your cursor over is suspicious, or selecting images and then asking Gemini to visualize them together. In practice, this feature isn’t very different from Android’s “Circle to Search,” which allows smartphone owners to use gestures like circling, highlighting, or tapping to connect with Google Search, which of course, also includes AI. While it’s a clever way to take advantage of the ways people use their phones, Circle to Search on its own didn’t inspire a mass exodus to Android. Similarly, Magic Cursor, as the AI pointer is called, won’t likely be a main selling point for the Googlebook, especially now that we have AI agents that can take over your computer and browser to do tasks for you. Google’s Rambler, however, may be one of the best of the AI features in this upgrade, as it meets a surprisingly useful need. However, this, too, isn’t enough reason to buy a new device, as it feels more like a feature that’s nice to have than a necessity. Vibe-coding widgets are a minor addition, as well. Other Googlebook features, like Gemini Spark and Gemini Live, also don’t require special, new hardware. What’s more interesting about the Googlebook is how it’s seemingly part of Google’s longer-term plan to capture a large part of the K-12 market that currently relies on Chromebooks, and push them towards the company’s Gemini AI. There are somewhere around 50 million Chromebooks in schools, used by students and educators, Google has said. In May, the company told TechCrunch that its current Chromebooks would continue to be supported, though many would become eligible to transition to the new Googlebook experience in the future. The $899 device will include 12 months of Google AI Pro, which gives you 5TB of cloud storage, Gemini Advanced tools, and 3 months of YouTube Premium, Adobe Photoshop and more. The devices will receive regular updates for up to 10 years, Google said. Googlebook pre-orders are open now on the Google Store, Best Buy, and other retailers. Devices will hit shelves on October 4 in the U.S., and October 5 in Canada, the U.K., Ireland, France, Germany, and Australia. Flagship devices in the new range are being built by Acer, ASUS, Dell, HP and Lenovo, with materials like aluminum, magnesium alloy and carbon fiber. They feature up to 2.8K OLED displays, haptic glass trackpads, and backlit keyboards. Under the hood, they’ll run on processors from Intel and Qualcomm paired with dedicated NPUs. Google says the device will offer up to 14 hours of battery life. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Corridor raises $25M seed to build a health benefits brokerage for SMBs
2026-09-21

Corridor raises $25M seed to build a health benefits brokerage for SMBs

TechCrunch | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt It’s been a while since Jackson Wagner left his job as a product lead at Scale AI in July 2022. Now, he’s building in a new space altogether: The new effort is an AI health benefits brokerage called Corridor that focuses on small businesses, which it says traditional brokerages often overlook because small accounts generate lower commissions even though they take the same amount of time as larger accounts. Corridor is betting an AI solution may help on that front. On its platform, customers work with human advisors while AI agents do administrative tasks in the background. The company says its agents can handle tedious office work like checking whether a doctor is in a customer’s insurance network, scheduling care, or providing a doctor updated health insurance information. “We are every single employee and management’s concierge for all things healthcare,” Nikhil Aggarwal, the company’s CEO, told TechCrunch. On Monday, Corridor said it had raised a $25 million seed round led by Bain Capital Ventures. Corridor came about after Wagner was in a running accident, and the poor handling of his care led him down a path of “significant, consistent, chronic pain.” “I developed a long list of complications that needed to be worked through one by one,” he said. He eventually left Scale AI to get a master’s degree in computer science and electrical engineering at UC Berkeley, before joining Build Robotics in 2023. Later, he teamed up with former Scale AI colleague Eric Qian to work on Capernaum AI, a startup building a clinical agent targeting musculoskeletal care and chronic pain. The two eventually approached Cold Start to see if they would fund Capernaum, but Aggarwal, who was a partner at Cold Start along with Jason Dong, saw a much larger opportunity. So the four of them took off and launched Corridor together. “Since health plans are the window through which the vast majority of Americans access health care, it became overwhelmingly clear that one of the highest impact things I could do to improve outcomes for individuals was to create a way for them to access better health plans,” Wagner said. Other companies in this space include Ignition Benefits and Nava Benefits. Investors in Corridor’s round include BoxGroup, as well as executives from OpenAI, Scale AI and Ramp. The startup is now preparing for Q4, since “80% of small businesses choose their health plan in Q4,” Aggarwal said. “Our vision is to build the most trusted healthcare institution in America,” he said. “To earn that, we are laser-focused on a simple promise: doing what we say we’re going to do for the businesses and employees we serve.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. TIME
Can ‘Pink Noise’ Help You Sleep Better?
2026-09-21

Can ‘Pink Noise’ Help You Sleep Better?

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When you’re asleep, your brain is still keeping tabs on the world outside. Sounds and even smells have been shown to alter brain activity during sleep. “Evolutionarily, it makes sense,” says Hong-Viet Ngo-Dehning, a lecturer in psychology at the University of Essex in the U.K. “There might be some danger, and we need to respond.” But some sounds might actually nudge us deeper into sleep. In a recent paper in Science Translational Medicine, researchers report that when they played a type of sound called “pink noise” to people sleeping in an fMRI machine, they could see the fluid surrounding the brain pulsing in response. That might indicate that such noises could provoke changes in the quality of sleep. The reason sleep feels restorative is still a subject of active research, but scientists are curious whether the movement of cerebrospinal fluid, which might remove biochemical waste products during sleep, could be involved. “A few years ago, we made this discovery using MRI imaging that when people fall asleep, there are these really large waves of fluid flow that pulse around the brain,” says Laura Lewis, a neuroscientist at MIT and an author of the new study. “Ever since then we've been really interested in understanding what controls these waves…and we think it would be really interesting to see if you could maybe make more.” For this study, Lewis and colleagues drew on prior research using “pink noise,” which is similar to white noise many people use while falling asleep but a bit lower-pitched and more even sounding. “It's less harsh-sounding than static white noise that people might be familiar with,” Lewis says. In 2013, Ngo-Dehning and colleagues found that playing pink noise to people during sleep seemed to improve their performance on memory tasks the next day. In the years since, findings have been mixed, Ngo-Dehning says, but he suspects that this type of noise does do something for the brain, at least under certain conditions. Lewis was curious to see whether bursts of pink noise, which have been shown in previous work to influence a type of brain waves called slow waves, would change brain fluid flow, too. Her coauthor graduate student Joshua Levitt developed an algorithm allowing him to play 50 millisecond bursts of pink noise precisely as slow waves peaked in the brain’s activity, while people were sleeping in an MRI—a tricky proposition, but one that allowed the team to see what the sound did to fluid flow in the brain. (Levitt and Lewis have applied to patent this technology and hope to commercialize it in a product that could be worn during sleep.) What the researchers saw confirmed what others have found: Pink noise amplified slow waves during sleep, potentially making sleep deeper. But they also saw that CSF flow increased in the wake of that amplification. “The most exciting part of our results, at least for me, was that we also observed an increase in cerebrospinal fluid inflow from the fourth ventricle into the brain,” says Levitt. “This was the first example that I'm aware of, at least in humans, of some stimulus being able to modulate cerebrospinal fluid flow non-invasively.” The study is exciting because it shows clearly that pink noise, slow waves, and these pulses of CSF flow are closely linked, says Ngo-Dehning, and it opens the door to studies where researchers intentionally boost CSF flow to see if it can help, say, people with poor sleep or people with neurodegenerative conditions that may be caused by impaired waste removal from the brain. “That would be the next step,” he says.

CNN, MS NOW, and Politico to Sue Trump Administration Over White House Ban

CNN, MS NOW, and Politico said Monday they will sue the Trump Administration following President Donald Trump's decision to ban the three media outlets from the White House. "This morning, we notified the government that we are filing a lawsuit today to protect our First Amendment rights and defend the principle that the government does not decide what the press reports or publishes,” the companies said in a joint statement. Trump took to Truth Social shortly afterwards and insisted the White House "is not instituting an assault on the free press" but is instead targeting "fake news"—something he argued is "totally out of control" and "a threat to our national security." CNN, MS NOW, and Politico said their reporters were denied access to the White House over the weekend and had their badges seized following Trump's ban issued Friday. MS NOW reporter Akayla Gardner on Saturday described arriving at the White House campus to discover her badge no longer worked. "The officer asked me to hand over my badge. He said that it was disabled. I asked why I was not able to get inside. He said it was above him," she recounted. Reporters from CNN and Politico shared similar experiences. “Without notice or process, the White House revoked our journalists’ credentials because it objected to our reporting. Left unchallenged, this threatens press freedom and the public’s right to independent journalism free from government interference," the joint statement from the outlets continued. CNN, which is among the five U.S. news outlets in the White House press pool for live television coverage, was originally due to cover Trump's trip to the U.N. General Assembly in New York on Monday. The other major television networks are standing with CNN by declining to replace its White House pool crew. The decision means the shared TV pool are not set to cover Trump’s events that have been designated for pool coverage. “This is to advise that, effective today, the TV pool will not be covering events designated as pool coverage of the President,” Bryan Boughton, chair of the TV pool and Fox News Washington bureau chief, wrote in a memo to the White House press corps, according to CNN. “This follows the White House’s position preventing CNN from fulfilling its assigned pool duties. There will be no replacement pool put in place. All other pool coverage will continue as normal, including Congress, VIPs, and selected events in Washington and around the country.” The Associated Press sent a note to media late Monday morning informing them of the changed plans. "Please be advised that no network travel pool is expected to be with U.S. President Donald Trump today as he heads to New York for the United Nations General Assembly," read the notice. "CNN was the designated network to cover the President's travel today, but the network has been barred by the White House." TIME has reached out to the White House for comment. Trump announced his decision to ban the three media outlets Friday afternoon in a Truth Social post that warned other news outlets could face similar restrictions. “Effective immediately, I am banning Fake News CNN, MSNOW... and Politico... from the White House as a result of their constant ‘reporting’ fake news!” Trump said. “Media Outlets shouldn’t be able to constantly write or report fiction and lies when they’re covering the President of the United States, the Trump Administration, or the United States of America.” When asked in the Oval Office later that day if the media ban is likely to be struck down in court, Trump responded: “I think it's good to point it out whether it survives or doesn't. I don't think that a court should allow fake news to be written day after day after day.” The ban has presented a new test of the First Amendment’s protection of a free press as his Administration moves to punish news organizations whose coverage he dislikes. The First Amendment forbids the government from "abridging" the freedom of the press—a principle intended to allow news organizations to scrutinize those in power without fear of government retaliation over their reporting. Trump has made several previous attempts to bar or restrict reporters from government spaces. During his first term, he revoked the White House press credentials of Jim Acosta, who was then a CNN correspondent, after a tense exchange during a news conference. CNN challenged the decision in court and a judge ordered the White House to reinstate his press pass. In early 2025, shortly after returning to office, Trump tried to bar Associated Press journalists from spaces where access is limited, such as the Oval Office and Air Force One, when the news organization declined to update their stylebook after Trump signed an Executive Order renaming the "Gulf of Mexico" as the “Gulf of America.” The Associated Press sued, and the case remains ongoing. The Department of Defense has also imposed various restrictions limiting journalists’ access in the Pentagon. In an unprecedented move in June, the department designated its press office a classified area. “The Pentagon Press Office has been redesignated as a Sensitive Compartmented Information Facility due to speechwriters from the Office of the Secretary of War sharing the facility,” said deputy Pentagon press secretary Joel Valdez. “As a result, journalists will no longer be permitted to enter the office space. There’s nothing controversial about that.” Trump has also verbally lashed out at several female reporters. Last month, CNN issued a statement in defense of its reporter Kristen Holmes, after Trump told her to "be quiet" and called her a "loud, boisterous person" during a tense exchange in the Oval Office. The White House's Rapid Response social media account later shared a clip of the exchange, referring to Holmes as “a disgraceful, humiliating embarrassment to her alleged profession.” In its response to the rhetoric, CNN said: “Public officials are free to challenge reporting they disagree with, but personal attacks on journalists for asking questions are beneath the office and inconsistent with the principles of a free press."
