Why K-Pop Took Off When C-Pop Didn’t
2026-09-21

Why K-Pop Took Off When C-Pop Didn’t

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings BTS performs at SoFi Stadium during their "ARIRANG" World Tour on Sept. 1, 2026 in Inglewood, CA. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings It’s not often that an 18th century folk song can be said to be the inspiration for a pop concert. But those who attended the finale of BTS’ “ARIRANG” North American concert tour at Los Angeles’ giant SoFi Stadium earlier this month will long remember the tens of thousands of fans who waved South Korean flags and sang along to the traditional Korean melody. The four-night run in LA was the finale to a tour of 31 sold-out shows, with combined attendance of approximately 1.92 million, according to figures released by the band’s agency. For comparison, Beyoncé’s 2023 Renaissance tour sold about 1.73 million tickets across 35 North American shows. The flag-waving scenes from the SoFi capped a banner year for Korean pop, following BTS’s World Cup final halftime performance. Earlier in the year, Golden, from KPop Demon Hunters, collected a Grammy, followed by an Academy Awards performance and the Oscar for best original song. Those performances and prizes will have brought smiles to the faces in Seoul’s foreign-policy circles, where there is a keen appreciation for the soft power that K-Pop brings South Korea. In Beijing, it will have led to much gnashing of teeth. For more than a decade, China has looked on with undisguised envy as its Korean neighbor has accumulated soft power through cultural exports. As far back as the 2014 National People’s Congress, the Communist Party heavyweight Wang Qishan marveled at the reach of Korean culture, citing popular TV dramas like My Love for the Star and the breakout dance hit Gangnam Style by Psy, arguably the first K-Pop star to break big worldwide. The interest went beyond public musing: Beijing resolved to learn from Seoul’s example. China’s culture ministry signed an agreement with its South Korean counterpart, providing for annual cultural-industry forums and cooperation in marketing cultural products abroad. But it is one thing for Beijing to study Seoul’s cultural outreach, and quite another to match it. China, despite possessing a vastly larger population, an extensive diaspora, and the world’s fourth-largest recorded-music market, has failed to produce a music machine comparable to the international following of the marquee K-Pop acts. And while Taiwan’s Mandarin-language stars and Hong Kong’s Cantopop veterans have their own histories, they haven’t as yet spawned an act as ubiquitous as Blackpink or Stray Kids. The audience Wang wondered about now extends well beyond America and Europe. Blackpink’s leading Spotify markets in 2024 included Indonesia, Mexico, the Philippines, and Brazil, spanning across the Global South, where Beijing has invested billions in the pursuit of soft power. These audiences' affection for K-pop reaches across linguistic and political boundaries, giving Seoul a welcome that its diplomats would struggle to arrange. One obvious reason for China’s failure to produce pop stars of global reach is the sheer size of the domestic market. “A concert tour of 10 Chinese cities is probably as big as if they go around the world,” Anthony Fung, professor of journalism and communications at the Chinese University of Hong Kong, told the Financial Times. Why spend years trying to understand Brazilian teenagers when there is so much money to be made in familiar surroundings? Chinese musicians have perfectly sound commercial reasons to focus on the home market, regardless of their government’s geopolitical ambitions. Korean companies, with fewer customers at home, had more reason to take on the risks and costs of finding fans abroad. That incentive would have achieved little without companies willing to adapt to foreign tastes. Korean agencies combined intensive training with production and promotion, recruited performers internationally, and hired foreign songwriters and choreographers. They built acts whose appeal survived the loss of the lyrics. For instance, fans didn’t need to understand Korean to dig Psy’s dance moves. The resulting music would not satisfy a purist’s definition of national, but its borrowings helped it travel: Korea’s entertainment industry learned to please people elsewhere. Those listeners could also help find the next audience. YouTube let fans share performances without waiting for local broadcasters to discover them; viewers could join in by making videos of their own. When Gangnam Style passed a billion views in December 2012, YouTube reported that it had already drawn at least a million views in nearly 75 countries. Fourteen years later, KPop Demon Hunters shows how much further this can go. The Sony Pictures Animation film distributed by Netflix made Korean pop culture its selling point. In other words, American businesses now have their own reasons to keep the world interested in Korea. Chinese performers, on the other hand, face greater difficulty getting that circulation started. YouTube and Instagram are blocked at home, separating their domestic following from the platforms where potential foreign fans congregate. They can release music overseas, of course. But their most enthusiastic supporters cannot pass it along as easily, so a hit on a Chinese platform has further to travel before it reaches a Mexican listener. Beijing’s determination to police what enters the country complicates its ambition to export what comes out. The policing also reaches into the relationship between performers and fans. In 2021, China’s internet authorities abolished online rankings of entertainers and tightened supervision of fan accounts. Broadcasting regulators banned idol-development shows and demanded political correctness from entertainers, expressing special disdain for supposedly effeminate men. Some restrictions addressed legitimate concerns about children being induced to overspend. But a state that prescribes acceptable masculinity is doing much more than protecting young consumers: It is claiming jurisdiction over the tastes that pop music exists to indulge. This is an expensive preference for a government seeking friends. Joseph Nye, the political scientist who developed the concept of soft power, wrote that “popular entertainment often contains subtle images and messages about individualism, consumer choice, and other values that have important political effects.” The messages themselves—lyrics about youthful love and angst—needn’t concern foreign policy to help a foreign minister. A country that others around the world have encountered as a source of pleasure has a better chance of receiving a sympathetic hearing when it asks for support. Seoul still needs persuasive policies, but K-pop gives it an advantage over governments whose principal introduction to foreign publics is a dispute or a threat. China itself has supplied a demonstration of how stubborn that affection can be. Beijing’s unofficial restrictions on Korean entertainment, imposed after Seoul agreed to host an American missile-defense system, have not prevented Chinese fans of K-Pop from traveling abroad to see the performers they love. Seoul has good reason to encourage an industry capable of sustaining such loyalties. Chinese officials might point to the Korean government’s support for entertainment companies to argue that the state has a useful role in cultural exports, but public assistance doesn’t entitle officials to supervise the relationship with the audience. Foreign listeners bring tastes no ministry can predict, and successful performers need room to accommodate them. China can afford the studios, the training, and the promotion. Its leaders must decide whether they can tolerate a pop star whose first obligation is to please the fans. Meanwhile, back in the US, there is every expectation that K-Pop will continue to break new ground in 2027: BTS and Blackpink feature prominently in speculation about who will perform in next year’s Super Bowl halftime show. The best Chinese officials can hope for is to score tickets to the game. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We Have a Climate Imagination Problem
2026-09-21

We Have a Climate Imagination Problem

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Earlier this year I stood in a draughty sports hall, facing rows of 10-year-olds sitting cross-legged on the scuffed floor. I asked them a question: “What does climate change mean?” Small hands shot up: “The sea rises and floods everything,” one child said. “Fires are gonna burn all the forests,” another added. “We all die.” The litany of apocalypse continued until I interrupted with a second question. “And what can we do about it?” After some shuffling, one girl tentatively offered: “Solar panels?” Then a louder boy called out, “Recycle!” His classmates nodded. I've worked in climate communication for nearly 30 years, interspersing sessions with CEOs, activists, and politicians with talks to school kids. Over those decades, I’ve watched the average 10-year-old acquire an increasingly sophisticated picture of the climate crisis they will inherit. They can imagine burning forests, flooded cities, dying species, and an uninhabitable planet. Many of them seem to understand climate science better than some CEOs. But ask these children to picture what we do about it, and suddenly their extraordinary imaginations contract to a recycling bin. To be clear, I am enthusiastically in favour of recycling—just not as our answer to a planetary emergency. But I understand why I’m still hearing that inadequate response. Since the greenhouse effect and global warming first entered mainstream public consciousness in the 1980s, we’ve offered people ever more detailed maps of the apocalypse with barely a sketch of the way out. The climate movement, of which I’m a proud member, has been complicit in this communications crisis. Most messages shared by progressive forces are merely an ad nauseam echo of: climate change is real, humans are to blame, and the consequences are catastrophic. I call this framing the earned dystopia, and it’s so ubiquitous most pre-teens can parrot it. After so many years, can we not imagine a different story? One reason for this imagination deficit is that storytelling has been treated as merely the spoonful of sugar to help the scientific medicine go down. Storytellers are asked to act as the marketing department for climate science. But if that subservient role for storytellers was ever needed, it’s increasingly futile now that climate change has started communicating itself. Our growing count of horrifying floods, fires, storms, and droughts capture headlines without my help. We have clear science and brutal direct experience of climate change, so why haven’t politicians and populations sprung into action? The logic of the climate crisis is clear. What we’re missing is the magic. Long before humans measured atmospheric carbon, we whispered tales of flood and fire. Stories have always helped us understand who we are and what we might become. Increasingly, researchers are proving this singular power of stories to spark ideas, possibilities, and acts of imagination. I believe part of this magic comes from stories’ purpose in curating emotions rather than evidence. Today, we don’t particularly lack information about heat pumps, electric vehicles, or regenerative agriculture. More details than you could ever want are just a click away. What we’re missing is an emotional architecture for change; how it feels to want a different future and to believe we can reach it. That’s where great storytelling could change everything. Our ability to enter a story and temporarily experience the character's world as our own is called narrative transportation. Studies prove that this experience of emoting along with fictional people can directly influence real people’s attitudes, intentions, and even our behaviors. Few other mechanisms can achieve the same effect. Climate politics has sensed this power, but translated it into the narrower language of “public narrative.” Politicians now plot narratives for electrification and renewables. Campaigners construct counter-narratives to fossil fuels. Brand teams test sustainability narratives against each other. In climate circles, everyone is searching for the breakthrough narrative that will finally unlock action. But too often the term is simply a cypher for messaging. And messaging is not the same thing as mythology. A political narrative tells us how to understand an issue while mythology helps us understand ourselves. And climate change is mythological in scale. It contains terrifying monsters, underdog heroes, unexpected allies, destruction and rebirth. Or at least it should. My problem is with the enormous cultural library of apocalyptic narratives compared to a surprisingly small shelf of fables that foreshadow a better ending. We need a plot twist in climate storytelling, moving beyond the threat and into the thrilling adventure of solving it. Tales of the messy decades in which billions of people change how they power homes, grow food, travel, build cities, and protect one another from a climate that has already changed Culture makers are beginning to catch on. I’ve been invited by television and film writers, social-media creators, and brands to help climate solutions appear in comedy, drama, adventure, and everyday life. Ever more box-office hits, like The Wild Robot, Avatar, and the upcoming Digger, reflect our fight against climate and nature crises. Sesame Street is helping kids cope with extreme weather. New climate fiction novels weave answers into stirring worldbuilding. These all work because they are compelling and moving stories above all, not climate science lectures dressed in a thin veil of narrative. Many other scriptwriters, novelists, social media creators, musicians and artists must add to this list. I passionately believe that climate solutions are the most important story of this century. When I return to that classroom over the coming years and ask the same two questions, the first answers will remain frightening. But when I ask what we can do about it, I hope every hand will shoot up, because we’ll have filled kids' imaginations with enough possibilities that they can start inventing answers we haven’t thought of yet. That will be a good day. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. We Need to Stop Talking Like Climate Change Is Partisan
2026-09-21

We Need to Stop Talking Like Climate Change Is Partisan

TIME | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This summer of disasters has shown us that climate impacts are not political, and it is past time we stopped talking about them as if they were. A flood doesn’t knock on your door and ask who you voted for in the last election before it sweeps your house away, nor does wildfire smoke distinguish between Republicans and Democrats before it chokes the air. People who raise awareness about climate change know that how you communicate your message matters, and there’s much to learn from them. We surveyed the top organizations and individuals who regularly communicate about climate change across the U.S. and Canada. Almost none said that advocating for candidates or policies was the goal of their work. Their top priority was helping people understand what’s happening and why it matters: we’ve always had heatwaves and droughts, storms, and floods—but the faster the planet warms, the worse these events get. Climate change is loading the weather dice against us, and its effects are growing. A recent study estimated that deaths caused by extreme heat are vastly under-reported. Air pollution, whether from burning fossil fuels or wildfire smoke, is responsible for a growing list of health impacts, from increased risk of asthma and dementia to premature death. The financial toll of climate change is also on the rise as climate-related expenses, including rising utility bills and insurance premiums, are already adding at least $400 to $900 to yearly household budgets. And as we have seen over the past few years, there are no climate havens: we are all at risk. Here’s the thing, though: when people connect what’s happening to why it’s happening, their support for all sorts of climate action grows. This fact is true in the U.S., in Canada, and in dozens of other countries too. Why? Because we need solutions at scale that make the easiest, most affordable choice the best choice for the planet and our future. This requires action at every level: individual, community, and corporate action, but also government policy that enables and accelerates the rest. Even as the impacts from climate change grow, data show that we still aren’t hearing or talking about it enough. Just under half of Canadians and 41% of Americans read or hear about climate change once a week. Only 20% of Americans discuss global warming with family and friends a few times a month or more. And nearly half, 44%, say they never discuss it at all. Why aren’t we hearing about it more? Why do so many articles about the devastation of the Nepal floods, the soaring costs of home insurance, or the escalating health risks of wildfire smoke fail to make the connection between these issues and climate change? There are many reasons, but one is the fact that polarized public discourse has made climate conversations more difficult, and even well-meaning efforts to “move climate change up” people’s priority list often fundamentally misrepresent the risks. The reality is that climate change doesn’t need to move up any list; it is already impacting any homeowner’s, farmer’s, teacher’s, or parent’s top concerns. But that connection isn’t always obvious: it needs to be made, again and again. Given this contraction in public climate conversations, we wanted to learn from those who are still doing climate communication. What’s working, and how can we amplify it? Here’s what we found. The first and most important lesson is that talking about climate change means talking about everyday life. When we asked climate newsletter writers, podcast hosts, people who regularly post on social media, and those building climate content for non-profits, their response was clear. Their first priority is to connect the climate crisis to other concerns people face: our jobs, our health, the places we love. They don’t set up climate change as a rival to those worries. Instead, they understand that climate change is already shaping the things people worry about. When we connect the dots between what we love and how climate change threatens it, it becomes obvious that nearly all of us have our own reasons to care and act. The second priority climate communicators identified was shifting public perceptions and norms. And this issue is equally vital. Three years ago, 71% of Canadians and 63% of Americans reported they were worried about climate change. But most people keep their worries to themselves and underestimate how many others share their concerns. When people don't hear their neighbors, coworkers, and friends talking about climate change, they’re less willing to bring it up themselves. Conversely, of everything people can learn about climate change, knowing that others care best predicts whether people speak up. And here's why it matters: if we don't talk about climate change, how will we know who else cares about it—and if we think we're alone in our concern, why would we act? Climate communicators are not pushing to make this a partisan issue—in fact, only 4% prioritized advocating for elected officials or candidates. Instead, they’re working to inform and educate their audiences about climate action, climate science, risks, and impacts, in hopes of equipping others to talk about climate change themselves. Climate communication that isn’t polarizing and connects directly to what we already care about is exactly what we need; we just need more of it. Yet only 41% of the communicators surveyed report having received any financial support for their climate communication in the past five years. A third work entirely alone, and two-thirds work either alone or with a small team of ten or fewer colleagues. Even with these limitations, they are producing content and getting the word out: nearly eight-in-ten say they’re publishing content about climate change at least once a week. We know that climate shocks, like the ones we’ve experienced this summer, open windows of opportunity: people who live through climate change-exacerbated extreme events are more supportive of climate science and climate action and are more civically and politically engaged. On this warming planet, we all need to learn from what the few who are talking about climate change are already doing. Research has shown that friends and family are trusted messengers who can initiate a pro-climate conversation loop. We all need to become climate communicators and stop letting those who want to delay climate action as long as possible polarize it and silence us. To care about climate change today, one doesn’t need to be anything other than a human being living on planet Earth. And when we stop talking about climate change like it’s a political issue and start talking about it as a shared challenge that we all need to work together to solve, that’s when we’ll finally be able to start talking about saving ourselves.
