Does James Talarico Have a Chance?
2026-09-21

Does James Talarico Have a Chance?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Also from Jim Newell, the Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. In 2018 a captivating congressman from El Paso nearly turned Texas blue. In a race against Sen. Ted Cruz that captivated Democrats nationwide, Beto O’Rourke lost by a modest 2.6 points, the closest a Democrat has come to winning a Senate or governor’s race in Texas in modern memory. Although this performance was still a loss that earned Democrats exactly zero Senate seats, it augured a bright future for Democrats in the state. “Blue Texas,” foretold since the Obama era, seemed on track. That perception grew in 2020, when Joe Biden lost the state to Donald Trump by only 6 points in a presidential year, improving 3 points on Hillary Clinton’s loss in 2016. The state’s color was turning. And then, in 2024, Texas Democrats, as O’Rourke told me, “got what they deserved.” Kamala Harris lost the state by a whopping 14 points. While the country shifted rightward as a whole, the turn against Democrats among Latino voters turbocharged the collapse in Texas. Cruz easily won another term in the Senate. “Blue Texas” had never seemed further away. Which makes it all the stranger that only two years later, we’re doing this again. Because Democrat James Talarico really could win a Texas Senate race. The political-media industrial complex has been down this road before. No one—no reporter, operative, commentator, analyst, or person on the street—wants to look like an idiot by overhyping it again. I certainly don’t. But after weeks of speaking with politicians and experienced campaign hands in Texas, there’s no denying it: There’s more to this race than the usual hopium. “You’d have to work pretty hard to not recognize that this is different,” University of Texas professor James Henson, director of the Texas Politics Project, told me. “That this Senate race is different than any Senate race we’ve seen for a couple of generations, let alone any statewide race.” He noted that in the run-up to the 2018 Texas Senate race, O’Rourke never led in any polls. Meanwhile, Talarico has led or tied in all but one recent poll. “I think Talarico is just one of, if not the, best candidates we’ve ever seen,” O’Rourke himself told me. Democrats have not won a statewide race in Texas since 1994. Especially given their setback in 2024, it was clear going into 2026 that Democrats would need a perfect storm to win. They just might get it. The national environment is at Democrats’ backs. Talarico is a compelling and well-funded candidate—albeit one not without baggage—running against a scandal-plagued and poorly funded opponent in Attorney General Ken Paxton. The strong gains Trump made among Latinos in South Texas over the issues of cost of living and immigration are reversing over the issues of … cost of living and immigration. There’s a populist revolt over data centers and Flock cameras. Gas is expensive. Everyone is mad. But it’s not just the conditions. There’s something more fundamentally different this time in Democrats’ approach. What struck me as I was talking to Democrats about this cycle is that, unlike in previous runs that had their hopes up, they’re not relying on vibes to do their work for them. The past failures and overhyping has chastened them and offered much to learn from, and they may well be learning it: They’re not promising a new era of Democratic Party rule in Texas. They’re just focused on winning this race, this time, and not squandering the opportunity that has fallen into their laps. Democrats’ future—not just in terms of winning control of the Senate in 2026, but in terms of staying competitive with Republicans in the long run—depends on their learning to win in red states. Have they finally cracked the code? Why does the idea of Texas turning blue hold such fascination in Democrats’ hearts? Why don’t charismatic Democratic candidates from Mississippi or Tennessee make the cover of Vanity Fair, rake in record amounts of money, and capture Democrats’ national attention? It’s not just because Texas is the crown jewel of red America, the second-largest state in the country with the second-biggest allotment of electoral votes. It’s because, unlike many other comfortably red states, there was a period when its demographics were shifting in a way that aligned with those of the Democratic Party. During the Obama years, the state was becoming younger, more concentrated around metropolitan areas, more diverse, and more white-collar. This was the coalition that Democrats were betting on for transformative change across the country, the party’s then recipe for an enduring majority. The idea, then, was not that Texas Republicans were going to convert en masse to voting for Democrats. It was that their majority would be diluted away as Democrats registered more and more Latino voters, assured to be on their side. College-educated transplants would flood to the orbits of Austin, Houston, and Dallas. Suburban voters would veer away from the GOP. The appropriate way to interpret this information was, and is, that Democrats had the ability to make Texas a competitive state. The inappropriate way to handle it was, and is, to think that assumed demographic change guaranteed that Texas would inevitably turn blue and it was only a question of when. This latter stance can breed an idleness that, as we’ve now seen, ensures it may not happen at all. One of the first tests of this era of demographic promise was the 2014 governor’s race, when Greg Abbott ran for election against Democratic state Sen. Wendy Davis. Davis had gone viral in 2013 for a 13-hour filibuster against a state legislative bill restricting abortion. She had a compelling biography, as someone raised by a single mother who rose to graduate from Harvard Law School. Helping her along in the field was Battleground Texas, an organizing group founded in 2013 by former Obama aides with the goal of registering Democratic-leaning voters in the state. While Battleground tried to explain that it was playing a long game, its linkup with Davis, who was attracting extraordinary national attention, applied pressure on both to show immediate results. But it was an awful election year for Democrats nationally, and Abbott defeated Davis by 21 points. “That was partly our fault,” Davis told me in an interview of the way expectations got out of hand. “Because when you’re running a campaign, you do need to build belief that it’s going to be different this time.” She didn’t have as forgiving a stance for the group that helped her along: “The unfortunate thing is there were a lot of big promises and hopes made by Battleground in that cycle,” she added, “that, when they didn’t come to fruition, some of the folks leading that effort just moved on.” Perhaps given that hangover, the O’Rourke campaign started off with low expectations in the 2018 cycle. “The O’Rourke campaign was not at first built to win,” Jeff Rotkoff, a Texas Democratic strategist who directed campaigns for the Texas AFL-CIO in 2018, told me. “In fact, they were dismissive of efforts of others to help them win.” Indeed, they were dismissive of Rotkoff’s own efforts to help them: He described to me how O’Rourke initially missed out on the AFL-CIO’s endorsement because his campaign hadn’t asked for it. The whole operation had a general “too cool for school” attitude early in the campaign, Rotkoff said, in which it rejected opportunities to work with the rest of the party infrastructure. But then it grew into a proper movement—not just among liberals nationally, but in Texas as well. And yet, to some degree, O’Rourke’s campaign was still winging it. Texas Democrats I’ve spoken to over the years have a way of framing O’Rourke’s 2018 Senate run as not only a highly impressive achievement … but also something they’re comfortable Monday-morning quarterbacking. There’s a pervasive sense that maybe he could have won if he’d just done a few things differently—though what those things are is up for debate. Part of the problem, everyone seems to agree, is that the party infrastructure itself wasn’t prepared for a competitive statewide race. The gubernatorial candidate, Lupe Valdez, “was sort of a sacrificial lamb” in the governor’s race against Abbott, as Henson put it. And the primary focus of the national Democratic Party was on flipping several suburban House seats, not on driving O’Rourke across the finish line. Rotkoff wanted to be clear that these weren’t attacks on O’Rourke personally, and that “they learned a lot of lessons” in real time. The point of comparison, though, is with the Talarico campaign, where “the strategic political analysis was there from the start.” This time, as former Texas Democratic Party Executive Director Manny Garcia told me, Talarico “doesn’t bear the burden of having to do it by himself.” Democrats have recruited a candidate for every state and federal race on the ballot, no matter how doomed the contest, to ensure that persuadable voters everywhere are hearing the message. (Republicans have derided this move as a gimmick.) The state party is cooperating with the Texas Majority PAC—in other words, with George Soros’ dollars—to provide resources. And Democrats have landed a strong gubernatorial recruit in state Rep. Gina Hinojosa, who sits next to Talarico in the state Capitol, to both challenge Abbott in a difficult year—making him work unusually hard for his fourth term—and reinforce the message of corruption the two candidates are using against their opponents. Talarico is “bringing so much interest to Texas, lots of money to Texas, and it helps me and my appeal to certain constituents. So we are complementing each other in a way that is very helpful for the statewide ticket,” Hinojosa told me in an interview. “There is this synergy happening up and down the ballot that we have never had before in Texas, and I do think that is a difference-maker.” So Democrats have the candidate. They have the environment. They have the opponent. They have the ticket. They have the money. They even have the strategy. One is hard-pressed to find a more dramatic shift in voter behavior between 2016 and 2024 than that of Latino voters in Texas. Hillary Clinton, per exit polls, won this cohort 61 percent to 34 percent in 2016. Eight years later—and with Donald Trump remaining precisely the same person he was, saying the same things he’s always said, every day, over the course of that stretch—Trump won 55 percent of Latinos in the state. Results on county-by-county levels are jaw-dropping. As the Washington Post reported after the 2024 election, 13 of the top 15 counties in the country with the largest rightward swings between 2016 and 2024 were in Texas. They’re all majority Hispanic. Inflation and economic hardship, which led to fond remembrances of the Trump economy from 2017 to 2019. Chaos at the border. Working-class Latinos joining the broader working-class turn away from the Democratic Party. Voters being another generation removed from their newness to the country and attaining a more conservative socioeconomic status. Social conservatives responding to leftward shifts among Democrats on cultural issues. Name something, and the answer is probably yes. This helps explain Democrats’ losses, from Davis to O’Rourke to Harris: The party may have gotten the demographic shifts it was expecting, but it never translated into the political coalition it predicted. As James Henson put it to me, “The growth of the Latino population relative to the Anglo white population was going to be a lot more complicated than the demographical-destiny trope really took into account.” And just about every Democrat and Republican I spoke to for this story said that Democrats took these voters, particularly in the Rio Grande Valley, for granted—“based on the color of their skin or their country of origin,” as Beto O’Rourke put it. A better way to put it, though, is that Democrats never had these voters secured in their coalition the way they thought they did. “Latinos felt for a long time like Democrats were the lesser of two evils,” Sergio García-Rios, an assistant professor at UT and director of polling for Univision, told me, “but that Democrats never deliver.” Democrats “thought, for a long time, that the only thing that mattered was immigration, to Latinos, and immigration reform.” But immigration reform never materialized, anyway. And then, after the parade of inflationary and border-security maladies under Biden, it was time to give something else a shot. “There’s a lot of people who are working-class in these areas who voted Democrat for a long time, and their life hasn’t gotten any better,” Chuck Rocha, a veteran Democratic strategist—who is advising both Talarico and Hinojosa this year—told me. “And then along comes Donald Trump, who sounds a lot like, if you close your eyes, Bernie Sanders,” Rocha added. “He’s talking about a rigged system. He’s talking about remanufacturing jobs in America. That’s kind of how Donald Trump controlled that space while Democrats don’t spend any money juxtaposing that position, because there’s no real party infrastructure in McAllen or in Brownsville. “And that’s the perfect storm,” he concluded, “that put us in a position where not all of those people automatically came on board like their mothers and grandmothers.” In the long run, Democrats’ ability to win back Latino working-class voters will hinge on their ability to win back working-class voters more broadly. But as far as 2026 goes, the upside for Democrats with Latino voters’ swingy voting preferences is that they can swing right back from Republicans’ 2024 highs. And they can even do so largely on the same issues with which Trump won those voters: the cost of living, and immigration. Manny Garcia, formerly of the Texas Democratic Party, told me that “people wanted something in economic terms that was being promised” by Trump. He added: “And right now, what people are seeing is, ‘Well, my prices aren’t going down like you promised, I’m not having a better economic outlook like you promised, and I am getting consistently disrespected. My family’s getting consistently disrespected. My community’s getting consistently disrespected.’ ” On immigration, Brendan Steinhauser, a Republican strategist and former campaign manager for John Cornyn, noted that Latinos in South Texas “want a secure border, don’t want the cartels to run rampant, we don’t like human trafficking, we don’t want to see immigrants dying in the desert of thirst.” At the same time, though, the current Trump administration has gone well beyond what they find acceptable. Now they also realize they “don’t want these excesses, don’t want the federal government to come in and round up everyone, especially people that don’t have a criminal past.” As Steinhauser put it: “There’s not a lot of support among Hispanic Texans in South Texas for doing mass deportation of people who are not violent criminals.” The polling shows clear signs of a reversion, with polls of Latinos in Texas backing down hard from Trump’s 2024 margins while Talarico enjoys strong support among them in his race against Paxton. A recent survey from Univision and YouGov showed Talarico leading among Latinos 56 percent to 37 percent. This flip is worth understanding specifically. Because it’s not that all of the voters Trump picked up in 2024 suddenly, in the words of Rocha, are “going to run up and become democratic socialists or start dyeing their hair blue or get a nose ring.” It just means that some of those who felt that promises made to them were broken could sit this election out. “Not all of those guys are coming back, and they still think that Democrats are queers or weak,” he added. “But they’re really pissed at Republicans.” As much as this is Texas Democrats’ best opportunity in modern memory to win a prestigious statewide race, it is, ultimately, only that: an opportunity. And James Talarico is a long way from winning this race. Talarico has been ahead in polls over the summer, with strong support from independents, but he’s rarely polling above 50 percent. The great question is whether Texas Republicans will come home for their candidate, warts and all. Their candidate has behaved confusingly. Ken Paxton has been running an anti-campaign at times so baffling that it’s worth considering whether his ability to get others to do his work for him is actually genius. To quote his felled primary opponent, Sen. John Cornyn, “Paxton’s a poor campaigner” who’s “lazy and doesn’t raise money. And he basically is not offering a very compelling case for his election.” As of the latest filing deadline, which covers the first two quarters of the year, Paxton had only $16.7 million (with joint fundraising committee hauls factored in), while Talarico had raised $72 million. Paxton was literally on vacation in London, famously the home of the British monarchy, with his girlfriend on America’s 250th anniversary. When he does bother to campaign, he makes peculiar choices, such as at a late August event for which he wore a Four Seasons Orlando–branded golf shirt and what appeared to be a $40,000 watch. All this, of course, is layered atop his history of having been impeached by the state House, having faced an untold number of corruption allegations, and having been divorced by his wife on “biblical grounds.” That he’s made it this far is because he’d remained in Trump’s favor after trying to overturn the 2020 election through a lawsuit hastily dismissed by the Supreme Court. What Paxton must have known, though, is that once he became the nominee, he’d be golden. National Republicans can’t afford to lose his race—outside money would have to swarm to prop him up. And, sure enough, the cavalry has finally arrived. After a lengthy finger-pointing match between Trump, Senate Republicans, and Texas donors about who was responsible for rescuing Paxton, they’re all chipping in. Between the recent commitments from Trump’s super PAC, Senate Republican leaders’ super PAC, and others—including Elon Musk—Paxton managed to close the spending gap with Talarico in a week. That money will allow Republicans to amplify Talarico’s most prominent weakness: his history of getting carried away during what is now called “Woke 1.0.” In 2020 and 2021, Talarico said that “God is non-binary,” that there are six biological sexes, and that “white skin gives me and every white American immunity from the virus” of racism. More recently, CNN uncovered an appearance by Talarico in a 2020 Sierra Club forum in which he said that he was “thankful that young activists have really drawn the link between climate change and White supremacy and capitalism in our country,” describing them as “oppressive systems” in need of “dismantling.” Talarico has tried to dismiss old comments like these as “cringey.” But with the right ad budget behind them, they could be just cringey enough to send Republican-inclined voters, noses held firmly, to pull the lever for Paxton. Then again, it’s quite something to see nearly $100 million in super PAC money dumped into Texas, in September of an election year, to save a Republican Senate candidate. The GOP’s late attempted rescue of Ken Paxton coincided with the Republican midterm convention in Dallas in early September, and Paxton’s role in this showcase was largely to serve as the butt of a joke. He himself gave lackadaisical remarks on the convention’s first night—alongside everything else, Paxton is also an unenthusiastic public speaker—to an indifferent crowd. For the rest of the convention, he was subjected to Trump’s regular insults about how he dresses poorly and looks bad. Paxton, waiting for his super PAC money to arrive, just stood there and took it. If Texas Democrats can’t beat this guy, in this environment, could they ever have a chance in Texas? It’s a question for Democrats in states across the country this year. Each red state in which Democrats are competitive in 2026 is a little different. Ohio and Iowa are less diverse but have more recent Democratic DNA in them, having voted for Barack Obama twice. Right now they’re being hit hard by Trump’s trade (and real) wars. Alaska is uniquely reliant on the federal government and has a large independent streak. What Texas, Alaska, Iowa, and Ohio all have in common is the national wind at their backs, strong candidates, and decisions by Trump that went out of their way to make life in their states more difficult. They also all prompt a lot of heartburn about races over the past decade that fell short despite initial signs of promise and alluring polling numbers the September before the election. Should Democrats get swept in all these races, under these dreamlike conditions, Democrats nationally may, once again, forswear ever getting emotionally or financially invested in these states. (Or at least for another two years, when they find themselves inevitably tempted again.) The issue is that soon, converting red states where Democrats have lost their foothold—not just fantasizing about converting them—will become a necessity for the party. Republicans already have a structural advantage in the Senate with their strength in small, rural states, giving Democrats limited options and narrow paths toward a Senate majority. And after the 2030 census, Democrats are going to need some fresh electoral votes. States like California and New York are projected to lose multiple congressional districts, while various other blue states will likely lose individual seats. This gives Democrats fewer Electoral College options and will officially retire the old “Blue Wall” pathway to victory of winning Michigan, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin (not that this pathway has been the most reliable recently). That means Democrats will need more options to win a presidential race. And there’s no better place to make it up—and beyond—in one fell swoop than Texas, to where many of those blue-state congressional districts will be transferred after the census. “We damn sure better be investing in Texas now than in 2032,” Wendy Davis told me. In other words, the long-term dream of “blue Texas” from the Obama years, seen as a way to lock in an inevitable, prolonged Democratic era, could soon become the medium-term need for “blue Texas” just for Democrats to keep up. The first order of business, though? Well, you’ve just got to win a race. Get the Surge in your inbox each Saturday. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork
2026-09-19

The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Gabfest Reads is a monthly series from the hosts of Slate’s Political Gabfest podcast. This month, David Plotz talks with journalist Annie Lowrey about her new book The Time Tax: How the Government Wastes Our Time—and How to Fix It. In this excerpt, Lowrey explains the “ordeal mechanism theory,” the decades-old idea behind why some government benefits are designed to be difficult to access, and why she argues it ends up failing the people who need help most. This partial transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. David Plotz: I had never heard of this concept in your book and I was like, ‘That’s an amazing concept.’ Can you just describe what the “ordeal mechanism theory” is? There’s some context in which I guess it could be right. But no, this turns out to be wrong because very often people who are very vulnerable, very low income just can’t make it through the ordeal. They don’t have phones, like up-to-date phones; they don’t have computers; they don’t have Wi-Fi; they don’t have stable addresses. Plus, their lives are really stressful, so it makes it really hard to get through things. But anyway, so that’s the ordeal mechanism theory.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?
2026-09-19

America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that welcomes Mitch McConnell back to the Senate. Is Lindsey Graham next? If there was but one thing that happened this week, it was politics. New Hampshire, sulky and brooding, thinks it’s too “cool” to participate in a Democratic-wave election. Did Pete Hegseth almost get impeached this week? A Texas Republican candidate caused a proper foofaraw after getting deeply upset by a photo of brown people enjoying a football game. And, please, we are still welcoming feedback on the changes around this newsletter. Let us know. But first: How much are these killer robots paying Donald Trump to like them? (Actually, that’s probably discoverable in public filings …) Last week’s calls from inside the house that A.I. companies are at risk of losing control of their monsters and killing everyone—true or not!—have continued to sweep the news. Congress rolled up its sleeves and got to work devising plans for slow-moving task forces that may or may not ever materialize, before leaving D.C. until November. The salience of A.I. concerns has skyrocketed overnight. Polls show deep concerns among the electorate about A.I. advances. This isn’t going to abate as an issue over time, either. With each passing day, A.I. will become a bigger and bigger part of everyone’s lives. Soon, you won’t be able to spit without hitting an A.I. agent coming at you with a knife. (Or, well, showing you a helpful recipe using ingredients you have sitting around the house.) In short, we’ve never seen a bigger layup issue for populists, left and right, heading down the stretch of an election season. Which makes it all the stranger that the guy who ushered in the new populist era, Donald Trump, doesn’t have it in him to say a cross word about these machines at all. He’ll only chastise those who do. “I am the Hoax Buster, and I’m right now breaking another Hoax—That AI is going to take over, consume, and destroy the World, and that Robots will be marching into our Cities, and getting rid of us all!” he posted this week. “This is even wilder than the RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX, or the Global Warming Scam.” In a separate screed regarding the need to regulate A.I., Trump posted that the “only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” He called the A.I. warnings a “SICK conspiracy” and warned: “Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Traitors, and Leakers, BEWARE!” All of this follows a late-August post responding to the data center backlash when Trump chastised voters for their concerns. “If we kill the Golden Goose,” he wrote then, “you will only have yourselves to blame.” Why can’t Trump just ride the wave here, when he’s usually so good at co-opting shrieking public panics? A few thoughts come to mind. You could say he’s genuinely concerned about being outmaneuvered by China on A.I. and that this is informed by classified briefings he gets on the matter. But he just doodles pictures of hamburgers during those. More important to him is that the tech people are nice to him, always bringing him gifts and purchasing protection by donating to his political action committees. What’s also important to him is that A.I. buildout has been responsible for most, if not all, of the GDP growth under his administration. The “Golden Goose,” then, is the Trump economy’s paper gain. It’s that last part that’s so critical given Trump’s psychology: He views the attack on the engine of growth he presides over as an attack on him. Comparing the A.I. backlash to the “RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX” is as close to a tell as there is. There haven’t been many bad polls for Democrats across the board since Labor Day, as pollsters shift to surveys of likely voters instead of registered voters and capture the Democratic-enthusiasm edge. (Required note of caution: Sometimes polls are wrong as hell!) But there’s one race that’s standing out, much as the likeness of an old man’s face once did on Cannon Mountain. Some recent surveys show Democrat Chris Pappas and Republican John Sununu, who served in the Senate previously, locked in a tight contest in New Hampshire’s Senate race. And this week, the Cook Political Report shifted its race rating to “toss-up.” What’s going on here? Sure, New Hampshire’s not the bluest state in history, but it should be comfortably blue enough for a Democrat to win in this environment. And Pappas, a sitting congressman, should be a perfectly acceptable head of hair to complete the task. The most obvious issue is Pappas’ opponent’s last name. The Sununus are one of New Hampshire’s royal families, and John’s brother Chris is a popular former governor. Republicans have also outspent Democrats in the race as they attempt something of a sneak attack, with plenty more coming in ad reservations this fall. Republican Gov. Kelly Ayotte, meanwhile, is cruising to reelection, and could carry Sununu along with her. We thiiiink this will sort itself out based on fundamentals, and that Republicans are mostly trying to get Democrats to spend money to defend it? But there are always anomalies in even would-be wave years, and Voters might think the Senate candidate is his brother is the stuff from which anomalies are made. It’s not a situation that Democrats can ignore anymore, at least. California Gov. Gavin Newsom told CNN’s Jake Tapper while fly-fishing (?) that he would not run for president if Kamala Harris ran. He wasn’t even backed into this corner. This was just two of the fellas out there fishing, and Newsom said it with barely any prompt. “Why would I?” Newsom said, after Tapper responded with a huh? “I wouldn’t do that to her, I wouldn’t do that to you. I wouldn’t waste everyone’s time.” What that tells us is that he must believe, or know, that Harris won’t run. Because there’s no way that Newsom is not running for president. No way! He also told Tapper that he would have to check to make sure a run would be OK with his family, but they all say that—the family is always reportedly enthused (unseen, off-camera). Another possibility is that Newsom will do a sneaky li’l thing like quick-announcing his candidacy before she has a chance to announce hers. Because he’s clearly thought about how to beat her. When Tapper said that the argument against Harris would be that she’s had her shot and lost, Newsom didn’t miss a beat. “Yeah,” Newsom said, “and, I mean, that would be the approach to the campaign, certainly. That would be the case we’d make. A pretty good one, actually. That’s objectively true.” Watch out for these sneaky Californians, folks. The Verge
Can John Ternus find Apple’s next big thing?
2026-09-21

Can John Ternus find Apple’s next big thing?

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Bloomberg’s chief Apple correspondent Mark Gurman on Siri, Tim Cook’s legacy, and what to expect from new CEO John Ternus. Today, I’m talking with Mark Gurman, who is the world’s best-sourced Apple reporter — both as Bloomberg’s chief Apple correspondent and the host of the upcoming podcast Power On. Earlier this month, Apple hosted its annual iPhone hardware event, virtually every detail of which was reported ahead of time by Mark. But this event was special, for a number of reasons. It was new Apple CEO John Ternus’ first major product launch since he took over for former CEO Tim Cook less than two weeks prior. It was also the debut of the iPhone Duo, Apple’s first foldable smartphone. The timing of those two things was not at all coincidental, as you’ll hear Mark explain. Apple takes these hardware events — and the stories they tell — extremely seriously. This year’s story actually says a great deal about what’s happening at Apple, how Cook managed in wholesale leadership ranks over the past year, and how all of that might change Apple’s culture. Furthermore, what all that tells about Ternus’ plan to navigate Apple from what you might call the iPhone era into the AI era. Okay: Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman on the future of Apple. Here we go. This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity. Mark Gurman, you’re the chief Apple correspondent and the managing editor of consumer technology at Bloomberg. Welcome to Decoder. Good to be here finally. Thanks for having me. And it’s been a while, so it’s good to catch up on all things Apple. Yeah, I’m really excited to talk to you. Obviously, Apple just had its big event. They announced the iPhone Duo. It was John Ternus’s first event as CEO. This is a Decoder episode, so we’ve got a new CEO, and we’ve got a new org chart inside of Apple. There’s been a lot of shuffling. You’ve been chronicling them very, very deeply over the past few years. Let’s just start with the event. I have a very basic question for you. Do you ever get nostalgic for a time when you didn’t know what was going to happen at the iPhone event? Do you ever wish that you, Mark Gurman, were about to be surprised? Honestly, I never think about it that way. I’m just fixated on delivering the goods and delivering the news. And people love their Apple. People want to know everything that’s coming down to the last minute, so I’m here to provide it for them. The thing with me, and I don’t know if most people know this about me, but I hope people know this about me, is that I’m a huge Apple fan through and through, and I’ve always been. And I’m so lucky to be able to cover a company that I love, a company that I am obsessed with, and a company whose products I use. There are so many people who cover companies that they couldn’t care less about. That’s not me. So I do what I do because I love Apple and I love covering Apple. I love the community around it, and I love talking about it. And it’s always fun to get a peek at the future and help keep not only myself informed, but people informed around me. That actually leads me to a lot of things because I’ve known you forever. We’ve been working in and around the same spaces forever. You got your start as an Apple blogger at the Apple rumor sites. There’s a whole culture around Apple that has grown up and changed over the years, and you can trace it to different eras of hardware, to different CEOs, to different sizes of the company. This was the first event with John Ternus as CEO. Apple announced a big new product in the iPhone Duo. Do you sense that the community is changing? How do you think that community has changed over the years? And do you think Ternus is going to lead to a difference in what that community feels like? I have to say, this event really didn’t feel much different than a Tim Cook event. They felt pretty interchangeable. It could have been Tim up there introducing it. It could have been Ternus up there introducing it. The one thing I will say that did feel slightly different is that Ternus came out swinging with a point of view on AI. The first thing he came out with was about the intelligent personal hub. Now, here’s the big difference between Tim Cook and John Ternus in terms of their management style. You’re inside Apple, you’re an executive, you’ll come to him with an idea, you’ll come to him with a viewpoint, a decision, an opinion, and he’ll just pepper you with more questions. He won’t make a singular decision in many cases. It’s very much communal decision-making, collaborative decision-making. John Ternus, if you come to him and you want him to make a decision, he’ll make a decision. He’ll have an opinion. Now, you may hate his opinion; you may completely disagree with him, but he has a viewpoint. But that’s the big difference between talking to people about Ternus and Tim. Ternus comes out talking about his view on AI. I may disagree with it. A lot of people may disagree with it. They may think the OpenAI or Anthropic approach is superior, but at least he came out and had a viewpoint. Apple’s been at this AI game now for two years since they launched Apple Intelligence at WWDC in 2024, and it’s been a mishmash of things. They haven’t really come out with a very strong narrative and viewpoint, and they finally did. So, as I said, whether you agree with it or not, they did do that. And I thought that was a really interesting way to kick off the keynote. All right, we’re just going to get into the weeds of Apple nerds disagreeing about things. I sat through that event, and I appreciated the same thing that you saw, which is, “Oh, we’re telling a story.” John Ternus is on the stage, and the first thing he’s doing is telling a story about this product and how it fits into the universe of technology. And then I took a beat, and I was like, oh, but they’re also just making sure that he’s successful at his first event by playing Apple’s greatest hits. Because an “intelligent personal hub” is just a replay of Steve Jobs saying the iMac was the digital hub. At the very end of the event, Ternus said, “There’s one more thing, and it’s the iPhone Duo.” There was just a part of all of that that, to me, felt like Apple saying, “Look, our new CEO can put on the clothes of the old CEO. He can do this same dance that you’re expecting.” Underneath it, what I felt was, to your point, that I don’t buy the story about the intelligent hub. Because any of the things we’re seeing AI do are happening in cloud services. It’s the same week that Meta launched Muse, right? You can feel what you want about Meta launching Muse, but Mark Zuckerberg is giving everybody 8GB of storage and 8GB of RAM in the cloud, and Muse can go do a lot of things that AI on the iPhone absolutely cannot do at this point in time. I just felt like there was a part where they were playing it really safe with John by having him basically show up and be the new lead singer of Journey. You can’t get it wrong if you’re going to do “Lights.” If you’re going to show up on stage and do “Don’t Stop Believin’,” you’re going to be fine for a minute. And it’s all the stuff that comes after that’s going to really show his real personality. But it sounds like you thought that that’s his point of view. Well, it’s a couple of things. At a very high level, Apple’s going to tell a story around what they have. If Apple invented ChatGPT, they would’ve invented ChatGPT, and they would’ve told a good story around that. Apple has gone as far as it’s able to, at least for now, on AI. So it’s telling the story, the best version of that story that they can with what they have to work with, with the bird they have in hand. So that’s the one thing. I agree with you on the intelligent personal hub, and how that compares to the iMac back in ‘98. And it feels like they’re trying to sell this pivot from the Mac as the center of everything with the peripherals around it; then you got to iCloud in 2011, the center of everything with the peripherals around it. Now, it’s this Intelligence right at the center with everything around it. But what’s so fascinating is that Apple held a major product launch event in September of the year 2026 and did not introduce a single notable new AI feature. The first 15 minutes or so of the keynote was simply a replay, a re-narrativization of what they introduced over the past two years with Apple Intelligence. And so they tried to just tell that story in a more concrete way. The other thing is they don’t have any AI devices at the very core, and so they need to prop up the iPhone as their AI device because they don’t have an alternative. But of course they’re only going to do that until they have that killer device, and then they’re going to change that whole narrative. So it’s a very narrative PR and marketing-driven company. And I’m not saying that to be critical. I mean, if I ran the company, obviously I’m going to tout what I have. I’m not going to veer from that. But it is interesting how they’re playing this. And I agree, they did play it very safe. There’s nothing safer than John Ternus introducing a new iPhone. It’s like the safest thing you can do. No major new AI product, no smart home hub, nothing in the spatial space, just straight down the middle. Yeah, it seems like that next wave of smart home products, which we’re expecting later this year... I say, “We’re expecting.” I’m expecting it because that is something you have reported. Mark Gurman is expecting new products from Apple. That is the first cut at some truly AI-native devices. Because you have Siri AI and it is functional now in a way that it previously was not functional, you can try some new classes of devices. There are about a half dozen AI-native devices coming in the next year and a half or so. The first will be the smart home hub, which is basically an Amazon Echo Show but Apple-style. It will have a square screen and an entirely new operating system. The idea is: what would a Siri AI operating system look like? This is that: intercom, video conferencing, proactive intelligence. So you walk up to it, it recognizes who you are, and it will show content personal to you. It will know who is speaking. Obviously, the Amazon Echo can do that, but Apple’s doing it in its own Apple-y way with AI-based Face ID. They’re working on a home security system. They’re working on another version of the smart home hub that I just talked about that is attached to a robotic arm. So you put it on your desk, and it can move around. It’s actually a category that’s pretty popping in Asia and in China right now. Then you’ve got the wearables, the smart glasses. I still believe those are going to be introduced and shipped in 2027, so not too far from now. Trust me, you’ll be wearing them. Within 365 days, you will be wearing them and have video footage of you wearing them at the Steve Jobs Theater. And if that is not the case, either something went horribly wrong for you or something went horribly wrong for Apple. You’ll also have the AirPods with cameras. That’s an interesting thing that we should talk about, but they’ll have the AirPods with cameras and then a few other wearables that I won’t get into. So this is what I was talking about when I said there were eras at Apple and people’s relationship with the company really changed. The Steve Jobs era was defined by devices, and in particular, Steve Jobs himself getting up and doing a keynote and famously putting up pictures of all the existing smartphones and saying, “Well, these all suck. And here’s the reason they all suck, and here’s the iPhone and here’s why it’s better.” That created one kind of relationship between Apple’s enormous community of fans, its leader, and the products. I think that was very successful. I think the Tim Cook era has been meaningfully different. I think it’s largely a story about scale. To some extent, it’s a story about values. To a huge extent, it’s a story about privacy. Apple has marketed itself as the privacy company. It fought the FBI to keep the iPhone encrypted. There’s a lot there with Tim Cook’s version of the company, but it’s more corporate. Certainly, it’s just a vaster company. It’s safer. It’s not as rough around the edges as Jobs’ Apple, but it wasn’t as device-based. I don’t think the Vision Pro has the same kind of mainstream cultural impact as even the hockey puck mouse that Jobs put out. There was a thing that he could do with products that Tim Cook Apple could not quite do with products. And that just feels really different to me. I’m going to correct you on that because every time I put an article or a post out about the Vision Pro being on life support, I get what amounts to basically a notch or two below a death threat from dozens of people. So people are passionate about it. Well, honestly, I think what it is is that people have created businesses around spatial computing and apps, and they’re just pissed off that they’ve invested all this time and money in a category that’s probably going to die out. And then you have consumers who spent four grand on this product, and they’re like, “I saved up for this thing, I’m living with this thing, and I’m pissed off to hear the realities.” But if you have another hour, we can talk about the Vision Pro. No, the comparison there is good. People have invested in it. They care deeply about it. The comparison I’m making is that the relationship people have to Apple in the Cook era is as an institution. It’s just like this big thing that spans the globe on a massive scale. Yeah, and in the Jobs era, it was all about really, really innovative new products that redefined categories over and over again. You’re describing a whole bunch of new products that came up in the Cook era that John Ternus is going to launch. How do you expect the culture of Apple to change around its products or its relationship to the culture to change as we go from one CEO to another? But think about what they did. You’ve got the coolest new iPhone and Apple product in maybe 10, 20 years introduced one week after Tim Cook steps down. And then for the next 12 to 18 months, you’ve got, I don’t know, six or seven new product categories. Maybe you have 25 pretty breakthrough things. So Tim Cook made a decision that, even though his tenure at a product company was pretty limited in major new things, the Apple Watch turned out to be a success, even though it started as a complete dud, as you remember. The AirPods are a unique beast in that they’re an accessory, but it’s not a platform on its own yet. It’s going to get there. And the Vision Pro is a failure. Tim Cook could have ended his tenure as CEO with the iPhone Duo. He could have just waited. Typical transitions at Apple happen mid-October or mid-April; they happen on January 1st. It was a specific decision that was made for John Ternus to be the one to introduce the iPhone Duo. It was a specific decision made for John Ternus to be the one to introduce this whole new product roadmap. Even though, as you know, because of the length of hardware development cycles and R&D, these things were in the oven for five to 10 years. So it’s really interesting that this decision was made to let Ternus be the face of this whole new era of products rather than allow Tim Cook to take the credit for it. And I think that’s to your point where they’re trying to draw the line in the sand of this new era. If Cook is already going to step down in the next three years, let’s just do it now and let Ternus take the credit for everything. Take me inside that. You’ve obviously done a lot of reporting. I think you were the first to break the news that Cook would be searching for a successor. You were, I think, among the very first to break that it would be Ternus. Tell me about that process. This is a big decision; Cook is going to play a huge role at the company. He has a massive compensation program tied pretty much to having to talk to Xi Jinping and Donald Trump. It seems like that’s his job: to just manage that relationship. For $47 million a year, I would talk to Trump and Xi Jinping on the phone every day. [Laughs] I would need at least 50, but that’s just me. That’s his job: to go do politics much the same way that Eric Schmidt at Google was in charge of governments after Larry Page became CEO. There’s a whole pattern here. Brad Smith at Microsoft, even though he wasn’t CEO, but yeah. He’s the president of the company. Yeah, there’s a parallel here. Tell me about this decision of Tim Cook wanting to elevate his position to something like that, and of making John Ternus the face of the product side of the company in this new role. So 2023 rolls around and Tim Cook starts every executive team meeting, all high-level meetings with people at Apple at the executive levels, with, “We need to start taking succession seriously. We need to start thinking about who the next people are.” At that time, there was a really strong realization that many of the people at the top of Apple either had half a billion dollars in the bank or were already billionaires, and that this era would start to age out really quickly. And so Tim Cook knew that he wanted to take a step back. Jeff Williams, the number two as the COO, knew he wanted to retire. The CFO, Luca Maestri, wanted to retire, too. The general counsel, Kate Adams, also wanted to retire. They were all about the same age, give or take a few years. So they basically came up with this plan that would go one by one over the next few years. It started with the CFO. Luca retires at the very end of 2024, and he’s replaced by the new CFO, Kevan Parekh. Then 2025 rolls around, and Jeff Williams, the COO, retires and is replaced by his deputy, Sabih Khan. Then in 2026, they had the transition for the general counsel, and they got a great replacement for her from Meta, Jennifer Newstead. And then at the end of 2026, the big one: Tim Cook to John Ternus. So they played this out very, very slowly. What Tim Cook has done, and I do think this is going to change in the Ternus era as we progress here, is that he came up with this strategy that I call “Hotel California.” You never want to leave. Tim Cook, he’s staying around as executive chairman. The general counsel had her stay around an extra six to nine months to run government affairs. Jeff Williams stayed around three to four months after stepping down as COO to just oversee the design team, the watch team, and the health teams as part of a transition. I think Apple, to some extent, is on such a high horse with its privacy message that it came away surprised that there would be some backlash to Apple doing this. They think, I believe, that people are overblowing it when it comes to Apple and everyone should just be focused on Meta. And just because Meta has done a poor job protecting people’s privacy doesn’t mean we’re going to. So we’ll see what happens. One thing: when I talk to the folks there, they never make the connection between Apple and Meta in the sense that Meta’s apps run on Apple’s phones. People are mad about Meta, and they still think the phones, Apple’s phones, are listening to them, and they can’t quite make that connection because it undoes a lot of their own messaging. If you believe your iPhone is listening to you, that’s not just Meta’s problem. It is also Apple’s problem. They kind of have to actively ignore that relationship because otherwise everything else falls apart. And I think that’s why there’s this blind spot. If people’s watches start generating transcripts, it’s exactly the same relationship as “my iPhone is listening to me, and that’s why I saw an ad for a vacation that I only talked to my friend about.” I’m curious what they’re going to do. I have a feeling they’re just going to bulldoze their way through it. They’re going to just try to tell a good story. They’re going to do a ton of PR briefings about it. They’re going to do a ton of white papers about it, and get as nerdy and in-depth as they can on this stuff. You know what they’re going to do? They’re going to throw out a lot of comparisons. I even heard Ternus do this in one of the interviews, in Apple’s statement on some of these features. I could totally imagine them saying, “Why is it any different recording from smart glasses if you could just hold your phone up and record people?” With the audio intelligence feature, one thing I didn’t even think of is the Apple Watch- It’s very funny you just mentioned that because that is exactly what Mark Zuckerberg just said to our friend Alex Heath. “How is it different from a phone?” This is the industry’s approach. I think the answer is that people are like, “Yeah, I can see you doing that, and I feel very comfortable telling you to stop.” There’s something different about that. I didn’t even realize this. Someone pointed this out to me the other day. It’s like your Apple Watch has had the Voice Memos app for, I don’t know, eight years at this point. Who stopped anyone from recording their whole day with the Voice Memos app on the Apple Watch anyway? This is just a more privacy-centric version of that. So Apple’s solving a problem here. They’re not creating one. So it just depends on how you look at these things. I think they’ll bulldoze their way through it. It’s going to be a problem, but I think they’ll be fine. All right. Last two before I let you get out of here. We’ve talked around the iPhone Duo a lot. We’ve mentioned it a lot. It’s the thing that just came out. We haven’t talked about it. What do you think of it? I think it’s incredible. I think, and this is a bold take, that foldables will become the new normal within a decade. If you look at the market share of smartphones at the end of 2006, smartphones had like 6 percent of the market. Now they have 85 percent of the market. It went over 50 percent of the market after five to six years. The iPhone itself was not the source of that, but the iPhone got everyone into the smartphone game. I think the iPhone Duo is going to do the same for the phone market. I think it’s going to become the new normal. Even though Apple is a late mover to foldables? Yeah. I think the Apple brand is that strong, really. I really do. I think the fit and finish, the software integration, is going to get people really excited about this category. I think that the Apple Upgrade program, the leasing program, will eventually expand internationally. I think that’s going to get people in it. I think that at some point they’re going to stop advertising sticker prices and focus on the monthly prices, and they’re going to get people locked into this thing. They’re going to have a family of iPhone Duos over time. You’ll see every phone maker just transitioning, transitioning to foldables. Samsung: half the line is already foldables. Very soon the candy bar style is going to become legacy. That’s a big prediction. Although I will say their iPhone handoff feature, where you can move a number between phones, seems to point to a future where I, for example, have the folding phone for work and then a much smaller, lighter phone for the weekends with the family. You can see that vision taking shape only because it’s now easy to port a number between devices, just whenever you want, which I think is incredible. Last question. We’ve talked a lot about Apple, its culture, and its set of products. What do you think its biggest challenges are in the new era under John Ternus, and what do you think its biggest opportunities are? I would say that I think the biggest challenge and the biggest opportunity are the same thing. Ternus’ Apple for the next three to five years is still Tim Cook’s Apple, right? That’s just the reality. He’s still going to be there in the background. He’s still going to loom large, and the product pipeline is still going to be coming out of an era that started under Tim Cook. But in five years from now, they are going to need to start thinking of what actually replaces the iPhone as the next thing. We went from the Mac to iCloud to AI as the center. What is Apple’s new thing after the phone? What is Apple’s new thing after the current generation of AI? And Ternus’ tenure as CEO, I think, will go down as a success if he’s able to find that next new centerpiece. He’s got a few years before he needs to start cooking on that, but I’m talking about something that can be in development for 5 to 10 years. Tim Cook never had the opportunity to have his new centerpiece of the company. He never had the opportunity to put his stamp on products at the company for two reasons. Both things that he tried to do as his centerpiece failed spectacularly. Obviously the Vision Pro, but the car — the car, that was Apple’s multi-trillion-dollar market cap opportunity, and they just blew it. Ternus will need to successfully hit that. I’d actually put a third thing on that list. It was the very beginning of the Cook era, with Jobs literally on his deathbed, and he said he’d figured out TV. He had to spend however long pretending he knew what that meant because I don’t think it had been solved. And so there was this first wave of “Apple’s going to redefine TV” because the last page of the Isaacson biography had a Jobs quote on it. They haven’t even done a new Apple TV box in four years. That’s a long time. It’s funny because you don’t need one. It never occurs to me that I need to upgrade this thing, but maybe there’s going to be some AI feature. Well, Siri AI. They’ve been waiting for the Siri AI update. This could be a whole other podcast, but Siri AI has been the gate to all of these new product categories. The AirPods were supposed to be out already, the Apple TV, the HomePod, the smart home hub… all these devices were supposed to come out. Apple couldn’t do any of them until Siri AI. Now Siri AI is here, so the floodgates are open. All right. Well, Mark, we’re going to have to have you back on the show to talk about those floodgates and all these new products very soon. I can’t believe you haven’t been on until now. Thank you so much for being on Decoder. Yeah. And obviously I’d love to have you on my new podcast, Power On Podcast, launching very shortly. So thanks in advance. Questions or comments? Hit us up at decoder@theverge.com. We really do read every email! A podcast from The Verge about big ideas and other problems. iPhone owners can now submit claims in Apple’s $250 million Siri AI settlement

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
The deadline to file a claim is December 21st, 2026. The deadline to file a claim is December 21st, 2026. Apple is paying $250 million to settle claims that it failed to deliver an AI-upgraded Siri — and now, eligible iPhone owners can submit a claim for a payout. If you live in the US and purchased an iPhone 15 Pro, iPhone 15 Pro Max, or any iPhone 16 model between June 10th, 2024, and March 29th, 2025, you can file a claim on the settlement’s website, as reported earlier by MacRumors. You can receive an estimated $25 per eligible device, but the settlement’s website notes that this can increase to $95 depending on how many people file claims. To file a claim, you’ll need to enter your name, contact information, and iPhone serial number on the settlement’s website before the December 21st, 2026 deadline. The class action lawsuit alleged Apple created a “clear and reasonable consumer expectation” that new AI features previewed during WWDC 2024 would launch alongside the iPhone 16. Instead, the iPhone 16 launched with a more limited set of capabilities despite being labeled as “built for Apple Intelligence.” The company later delayed the rollout of its AI-upgraded Siri, which only just arrived with iOS 27 earlier this month. Apple has denied any wrongdoing. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

The X500, X500 Pro, and X500 Pro Max launch this month in China, with a global release on the way later.﻿ The X500, X500 Pro, and X500 Pro Max launch this month in China, with a global release on the way later.﻿ Vivo’s new X500 flagship phones have arrived in China, and as usual the company’s focus is firmly on photography. The X500 Pro Max — a new tier in its lineup — is the first phone to use new sensors and silicon from Sony and MediaTek that enable impressive dynamic range and slow-motion video recording. The X500 Pro Max uses Sony’s new Lytia L910 image sensor, its first to use LOFIC, a sensor technology that enables expanded dynamic range. The 50-megapixel, 1/1.28-inch-type sensor delivers what Vivo calls “true 17 EV ultra-high dynamic range,” more than the 16.5 stops claimed by the Xiaomi 17 Ultra. The excellent 200-megapixel, 3.7x telephoto from the X300 Ultra returns here too, along with support for that phone’s 400mm teleconverter lens. The X500 Pro and Pro Max are also the first phones run on MediaTek’s new Dimensity 9600 Pro, with the 9600M found in the base X500. The 2nm Pro chip brings with it the usual performance bumps, but also the ability to record 4K 240fps slow-motion video. 10bit Log recording and new cinematic video styles are included too. Display sizes range from the compact 6.36-inch Pro to the 6.85-inch Pro Max, with the base X500 in-between. All three phones feature OLED displays, silicon-carbon batteries between 6,510mAh and 8,000mAh in size, and run on Vivo’s OriginOS 7 software. Today’s launch is China-only, but a global launch is coming, though we don’t yet know when. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. These are the first five Googlebook laptops
2026-09-21

These are the first five Googlebook laptops

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Google’s promising up to 10 years of updates for the new Googlebooks from Acer, Asus, Dell, HP, and Lenovo. Google’s promising up to 10 years of updates for the new Googlebooks from Acer, Asus, Dell, HP, and Lenovo. Google and partners are finally announcing the first laptops under the new Googlebook banner. There are five models from Acer, Asus, Dell, HP, and Lenovo to kick things off. They’re available for preorder starting today and arrive in stores on October 4th. Starting prices range from $899 to $1,299, putting the Googlebooks in the same territory as the MacBook Air and a plethora of Windows laptops. Each Googlebook also comes with 12 months of Google AI Pro with 5TB of cloud storage, along with three-month trials of YouTube Premium, Adobe Photoshop, and CapCut. Google’s also promising up to 10 years of updates. Here’s a look at the hardware each Googlebook has to offer for those prices, and how they compare to each other. The Acer Googlebook 14 is the only convertible 2-in-1 in the first round of Googlebooks, featuring a design a bit like Acer’s Chromebook Plus Spin 714. Acer says it offers up to 16 hours of battery life. It also currently has the lowest starting price at $899, although that might not last — Acer’s Kelly Odle said in a comment to The Verge that $899 is the laptop’s “initial launch price available for a limited time,” adding that Acer plans to “reevaluate pricing over time based on component supplies and other market pressures.” Ongoing shortages of RAM and other components have been driving up prices on other laptops, like Apple’s MacBook Neo, and unfortunately it sounds like a price hike isn’t out of the question for the Acer Googlebook 14, either. Asus’ Googlebook 14 takes after its ExpertBook Ultra with its design, featuring a metallic gray-blue chassis and more ports than most of the other Googlebooks. It’s the lightest of the first five models at 2.2 pounds, although it isn’t the thinnest (that title goes to Dell). The Asus Googlebook 14 is one of only two that come with an HDMI port as well. It’s one of the pricier Googlebooks, starting at $1,299, but it features spec options similar to the others. Dell’s first Googlebook, starting at $1,199, closely resembles the XPS 13 but with a light gold “shimmer” colorway, the Googlebook glowbar, and Google’s software. Unlike the Windows XPS 13, the XPS Googlebook also has a fingerprint reader. Unfortunately it’s just as sparse on ports as the original, with only two USB-C ports. The HP Googlebook 14 starts at $1,299 and notably includes both facial recognition and a fingerprint reader. It also currently has the highest storage capacity on a Googlebook, maxing out at 1TB. It sports a dark blue chassis similar to the HP Spectre, but it’s light on ports with just two USB-C ports and a headphone jack. Lenovo’s Googlebook starts at the higher end at $1,299, but its 15.3-inch display is the largest display out of the first five models. It’s one of the only ones with an HDMI port, and it’s also the only one with a white chassis, which has a satin-like finish to it, rather than metallic blue or gold. Lenovo’s also launching an AI mouse alongside the Googlebook 15 with a matching glowbar. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Verge
The long dream of the Googlebook
2026-09-21

The long dream of the Googlebook

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Google’s latest attempt to reinvent the laptop will require everything the company does well — and a few things it doesn’t. Google’s latest attempt to reinvent the laptop will require everything the company does well — and a few things it doesn’t. In late 2010, an up-and-coming Google executive named Sundar Pichai took the stage in front of a bunch of reporters and explained that Google had a big new idea about laptops. In the future, he said, the only thing you’d need on your computer was a web browser — that was where all your apps, data, and connections would live going forward. Your computer didn’t need much storage, Pichai explained, or any fancy hardware, because the cloud would do the work. The software designed for these new laptops, Chrome OS, was an enormous bet that the web would eventually overtake everything. Chrome OS (which eventually became ChromeOS) has been, in many ways, a huge hit. But the computer currently on Rick Osterloh’s desk isn’t a Chromebook. Osterloh is Google’s head of hardware and devices, and the sleek, slim device he’s tapping on as he joins our Google Meet is a 13-inch Dell clamshell with decidedly un-Chromebook specs: 16 gigs of RAM, a cutting-edge Qualcomm processor, a light and thin and MacBook-ish metallic look. It’s called the XPS Googlebook, and it’s one of five new devices running Googlebook OS, Google’s latest big idea about computers. At first blush, Googlebooks feel like classic Google in a bad way: yet another operating system, yet another brand, yet another thing destined for the Google graveyard. But Osterloh sees it differently. He greenlit the whole project in part because he saw it as “the ultimate focusing move” for Google. For a long time, he says, Google has built, maintained, and shipped two operating systems. ChromeOS stayed on laptops, while Android conquered phones and tablets and TVs and cars and everything else. Slowly but surely, Google has built Android into a familiar interface and a large app ecosystem across all those devices. Except for laptops. Meanwhile, Google became convinced that the advent of AI might change the way we use computers forever — but might not need to change the computers themselves. Osterloh says he has tables full of wheel-reinventing prototypes, and of course the company is betting big on things like smart glasses, but high-end PCs, which have the power needed to run AI models for coding, dictation, and more, have been a surprise winner of the AI revolution so far. (Just ask Apple, which has seen AI enthusiasts buy high-end Macs in huge volume.) “I think laptops are one of those resonant form factors,” Osterloh says. “It’s an established category that’s incredibly useful for productivity and apps, and it’s going to be really hard to replace that.” Those two ideas together — Android’s dominance and the rise of AI — tell the whole story of Googlebook. There are a lot of bets inside of the Googlebook project, both about what we do on computers and how we’ll do it going forward, but maybe the most surprising is Google’s commitment to building kickass laptops. A generation ago, then-CEO Eric Schmidt argued that the future was “disposable” hardware, that devices wouldn’t matter as the internet took over. Googlebooks represent Google’s growing belief that devices matter more than ever. And that a good laptop is worth paying for. “The key thing that had changed was just the rapid pace of evolution that Android itself has gone under.” The Googlebook project started in earnest a couple of years ago, but some of the ideas have been floating around much longer. Rumors and reports of a ChromeOS-Android merger have been around for more than a decade, and Google has tried various ways to bring the two operating systems into harmony. The company spent years programming ChromeOS to be able to run Android apps (and even Windows apps), but never nailed the integration. For years, a project called Fuchsia looked like the combination users had been waiting for, but that petered out into a smart home-specific project. In early 2024, though, a couple of Googlers asked a new question: What if, instead of trying to bring Android apps to ChromeOS, we brought the best of ChromeOS to Android? They weren’t the first people to ask that question, but they were the first to time it right. “The key thing that had changed,” says Prajakta Gudadhe, Google’s VP of engineering for laptops and tablets, “was just the rapid pace of evolution that Android itself has gone under.” For so many years, Android was a phone operating system that was sometimes hamfistedly shoved into other devices. But Gudadhe felt like Android had finally matured into something truly cross-platform. Developers had begun to embrace multiple screen sizes and orientations, and the Android team at Google had changed the way they built the operating system. “Android at that time was serving multiple form factors, from phones to wearables to TVs to autos,” Gudadhe says, “and because of that they just had to invest in a much more modular stack that could support this diversity.” What that meant in practice was that it might finally be possible to build an Android laptop that could natively run the universe of Android apps, without looking and working like a grossly oversized Android phone. By combining base Android technology with some of the best features of ChromeOS — excellent multitasking and multi-window support, fast boot times, easy software updates — Google could have the best of both worlds. The idea became a memo proposing a new laptop OS, which was excitedly received by Google leadership, and Project Aluminium was born. (To be clear: It is A-lu-min-i-um, not aluminum, and there was a swear jar in the office for people who dared to pronounce it the American way.) In addition to the technical underpinnings, the Aluminium pitch included a couple of big ideas about the future of laptops. The first was that more and more users were starting their computing journey with Android devices, and thus were expecting all their other computers to both interact with and even look like their Android phones. They wanted widgets, homescreens, notification shades, and customization options. They also wanted all their apps on their laptop, and their data in perfect sync all the time. That became one of the core tenets of the operating system — perfect integration with Android devices. (iPhone owners were, and are, mostly out of luck on this front.) The team was also convinced that Gemini and other AI tools were part of the future of computing, and that they should be built into the operating system and not just a separate app. The advent of vibe coding meant people might want to build their own software, in a terminal or with the help of on-device agents, and would surely want easier ways to get it running on their devices. But even little things about computer use felt ripe for AI change. “The mouse pointer? That thing seems pretty stupid,” says Alexander Kuscher, Google’s senior director of product management for laptops and tablets, just naming one example. Your cursor represents your attention, and moves around with your brain and your eyes, so why can’t your computer just see and respond to it? “I’m copying and pasting stuff all over the place, and typing to chatbots that don’t give me the ability to just express my needs with the mouse pointer.” That thinking became one of the most novel features of Googlebook OS, Magic Pointer, which lets you invoke your AI bot just by wiggling your cursor over an image or a bit of text. The idea, Kuscher said, was to “not change the ergonomics [of a laptop], but to make sure we take care of people coming from the ecosystem, living in an AI world.” As the Aluminium team started working on an AI-centric, Android-powered laptop, they began discovering problems — and other teams at Google trying to solve them. “One is Android Automotive, which cares a lot about multi-user,” Kuscher says. “They need fast user switching because different people get in the car. We were like, oh, me too!” They found folks on the Android Wear team working on improving the Bluetooth stack for better accessory connectivity, and people on the XR team thinking about multi-window support for Android. This is the focusing that gets Osterloh excited. The well-earned stereotype about Google is that nobody ever talks to anybody else, and so the company ships a thousand slightly different messaging apps, reminders systems, and AI subscriptions. In that vein, the Aluminium team could have come up with new ideas about everything and baked them all into ChromeOS; what they did instead was integrate so many disparate ideas about Android into a single platform. Even the ultimate name of the devices and the software, Googlebook and Googlebook OS, hint at the extent to which Google hopes Aluminium brings everything Google-y together in one place. “We realized pretty quickly that this is about the whole Google experience,” Kuscher says. “If you have a phone that runs Google, and a car, and a laptop, that’s what this is about.” (The naming here is a delightful bit of Google chaos, by the way. Googlebooks run Googlebook OS, which is mostly Android, but with some ChromeOS trappings thrown in. Get it? Me neither. They’re Android laptops.) It became very clear to the Googlebook team that this operating system was going to ask a lot of the hardware it ran on. All the system-level AI would need enough processing power and memory to feel seamless, of course, but it went further than that. “We didn’t want to have Magic Pointer, and then it’s really hard on your trackpad to actually use it,” says Kuscher. “That’s just incongruent.” Likewise, what good is letting you build software with a prompt if the keyboard is awful to type on or the battery dies before it’s done? If the screen is too small, or too low-res, nobody will want to use it instead of their phone. For a Googlebook to be even a little bit worthwhile, the hardware would need to be great. From the beginning, Osterloh says, Google wanted to build an ecosystem of Googlebooks. The company began reaching out to PC makers, chip companies, and even phone manufacturers, all with roughly the same pitch: You’ve already been making either great laptops, great Android devices, or both, and now we’re putting the whole puzzle together. For a PC industry always looking for Windows competition and increasingly interested in building ecosystems of products to compete with Apple, the pitch worked: Google is starting with five devices from five partners — Acer, Asus, Dell, HP, and Lenovo — and Osterloh says there are many other partners waiting in the wings. Potentially including Google’s own Pixel team. The first five devices, he said, are merely the ones that are ready now. And he says partners are clamoring to be next. “I still fundamentally believe it will always be your central computer.” The harder group to convince of this whole thing? Users. For all their success in education and elsewhere, Chromebooks never really caught on as personal computers. That’s largely Google’s fault, and largely for two reasons: because Google never successfully convinced people that all they needed was a web browser, and because Google and its partners spent so many years making and marketing crappy computers. Early Chromebook commercials were, in fact, explicitly anti-hardware: They showed people lighting their laptops on fire and throwing them into rivers, because who cared about the device when everything was in the cloud? As a result, even when Google and its partners have made high-end Chromebooks, like the terrific Pixelbook, they failed to convince anyone that they were worth the money. The first lineup of Googlebooks will cost between $899 and $1,299, which puts them squarely in competition with devices like the MacBook Air. Osterloh says he thinks Googlebooks will eventually reach other form factors and lower prices, but seems unfazed by the task of convincing people to spend top dollar on a Google laptop. For one thing, he thinks the increasing popularity of AI has made people more willing to buy high-end computers in general, and has maybe even increased the importance of laptops and desktops. “I still fundamentally believe it will always be your central computer,” he says. “The thing that connects you to networks, that has the primary brain, the important screen for entertainment and productivity.” But more than that, he agrees that this kind of hardware, and this kind of price tag, are simply what it requires to do Googlebooks well. And Google definitely won’t get a third chance to convince you it’s right about laptops. When Google first announced the Googlebook concept in May, it was heavily focused on AI and Gemini. But the work that led to Googlebooks goes back much further than the company’s current obsession with LLMs, and is about more than Google’s chatbot. In some ways, Osterloh says, the whole history of Android has led up to the launch of Googlebooks. Their first job, as he sees it, is to “run the applications you know how to use and sync really well with all your Android usage and applications and devices that you’ve connected to it. And it just happens to be an awesome laptop.” Sixteen years ago, Google shipped a laptop for the internet age. Now it’s trying to build one for the mobile era that has already come, and the AI-centric one that’s still forming. Getting it right will require marshaling nearly everything Google does well — and the best devices it has ever made. It will also require proving to users that they need a computer that’s more like their phone, and more connected to their AI ecosystem. None of that is a given; it might be an even bigger leap than trying to move your laptop to the web. But if Google’s bet pays off, our cursors might never wiggle the same way again. The Download: investigating deaths at the US border’s “virtual wall”
2026-09-21

The Download: investigating deaths at the US border’s “virtual wall”

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. When José Morales Bernal crossed the border into the US in April 2024, the day before his 32nd birthday, he was within range of three surveillance towers equipped with cameras and AI to automatically detect and track people. If the system worked as intended, Morales should have been apprehended. If he needed medical help, agents were trained to provide it. None of that happened. Instead, Morales died just 360 feet from the closest tower. It was local landfill workers, rather than Border Patrol, who first spotted him. An autopsy concluded that he had died of “environmental exposure.” A first-of-its-kind investigation by MIT Technology Review reveals that deaths like Morales’s are startlingly common. After mapping nearly 4,000 locations where human remains were found against information on nearly 600 surveillance towers, we discovered that a humanitarian crisis at the border has unfolded in view of the government’s own cameras. Read our full investigation into the deadly failings of the virtual wall. All of these stories are part of Dying on Camera, our new series investigating the failures of border surveillance technology and their human cost.
